Classes
-------

.. py:class:: AntisymmetrizerExpansion

    Lower a contraction's permutation operator into an explicit contraction plus one permuted accumulation per term.

    A spec may name a permutation operator (``PermutationOperator``), as in

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::einsum("i,j,a,b <- P(ij) P(ab) i,m,a,e ; m,b,e,j", 1.0, &r2, 1.0, t2, W);

    and a node carrying one already computes the right answer without this pass:
    ``dispatch::string_einsum`` contracts into a temporary and accumulates the
    terms out of it. What it cannot do is own that temporary. It is a
    ``std::vector`` sized from C on EVERY call, which for an :math:`o^2v^2` residual
    term is an allocation per replay and, at production dimensions, an allocation
    far too large to make per replay.

    This pass rewrites the site into nodes the graph owns:

    .. code-block:: text

       einsum(spec without the operator)  -> tmp     // tmp = ab_pf * (A (x) B)
       permute(term_0 <- c_indices)       -> C       // C = c_pf * C + sign_0 * tmp
       permute(term_t <- c_indices)       -> C       // C = C + sign_t * P_t(tmp)

    The temporary becomes an ordinary intermediate, so Materialization,
    MemoryPlanning and the lifetime passes treat it as they treat every other one,
    and the allocation happens once instead of per call.

    The contraction cannot write C directly: the permuted accumulations would then
    read what they are writing. C's own prefactor rides on the FIRST term, which
    is what keeps it applied exactly once rather than once per term, and the
    identity term is always first so that term is the one that may overwrite.

    **What this does not do**

    A ``OpKind::Permute`` node carrying an operator is LEFT ALONE. It needs no
    temporary, because every term reads a source the operation never writes, so
    lowering it would replace one node with N that do exactly the same work in the
    same order. That is a node-count regression with nothing bought: the N
    permutes share one source and one destination, so there is nothing for CSE to
    merge and nothing for PermuteFusion to fold. The whole-tensor (T)
    antisymmetrizer in ``examples/toy/ccsd_t_spinorbital_toy.py`` is the case,
    where it would turn two nodes back into eighteen.

    **Ordering**

    Early in the graph-transforming group, ahead of ConstantFolding, so everything
    downstream sees the lowered form: CSE and DeadNodeElimination over the emitted
    nodes, LoopInvariantHoisting over the temporary's construction, and
    MemoryPlanning over its lifetime. Recurses into subgraphs, because a CCSD
    residual is captured as a loop body.

    **Example**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("ring");
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           cg::einsum("i,j,a,b <- P(ij) P(ab) i,m,a,e ; m,b,e,j", 1.0, &r2, 1.0, t2, W);
       }
       graph.apply(cg::PassManager::create_default());
       // one einsum node becomes an einsum into scratch plus four accumulating permutes


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_sites
        :type: int

        Einsum nodes found carrying at least one permutation operator.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_expanded
        :type: int

        Sites actually rewritten: those that also passed the operand gate (one
        dtype, a rank-erased impl on every operand, known extents for the
        temporary).


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_terms
        :type: int

        Permuted accumulations emitted across every rewritten site, which is the
        sum of the sites' term counts.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: AntisymmetrizerFolding

    Collapse a permutation operator contracted against an antisymmetric operand into a scalar multiple.

    When :math:`W` is antisymmetric under every permutation :math:`P[G]` sums over,

    .. math::

       \sum_x W(x)\,P[G](V)(x) \;=\; N \sum_x W(x)\,V(x)

    with :math:`N` the operator's term count. Substituting :math:`y = \sigma_t(x)`
    turns each term into :math:`\mathrm{sign}_t \sum_y W(\sigma_t^{-1}(y))V(y)`, and
    :math:`W`'s antisymmetry makes that :math:`\mathrm{sign}_t^2 \sum_y W V`, so every
    term contributes the same thing.

    The saving is the whole antisymmetrizer: :math:`N-1` permuted accumulations over
    a tensor the size of the contraction's operands, replaced by one scalar
    multiply. It is the transformation
    `examples/toy/ccsd_t_spinorbital_toy.py` performs by hand between its "naive"
    and "folded" (T) spellings, where its own table puts it at about a third of the
    whole-tensor runtime.

    **What it matches**

    - a ``OpKind::Dot`` whose operands are ``A`` and ``B``;
    - ``B`` written by exactly one node, which carries an operator with a ZERO destination prefactor;
    - ``A`` carrying a ``TensorHandle::symmetry_hint`` that states antisymmetry on every axis the operator permutes;
    - nothing between the two nodes writing either operand.

    The rewrite repoints the contraction at the operator's SOURCE and inserts a
    scale by :math:`N\alpha` on the result, with :math:`\alpha` the operator node's
    own scale (:math:`B = \alpha P[G](V)`), conjugated when the contraction is a
    ``dotc`` and ``B`` is its conjugated operand. A real result under an imaginary
    :math:`\alpha` is left alone. The operator node is left standing;
    ``DeadNodeElimination`` removes it when nothing else reads it, which in the
    toy's naive (T) is true of ``V`` and false of ``W``, matching by construction
    what the hand-written folded spelling does.

    **Where the premise comes from**

    ``AntisymmetryInference``, which must run first, and behind it
    ``AntisymmetryDetection`` for any premise that is not structural. Together
    they reach both arms: an operator whose groups are all singletons carries its
    antisymmetry unconditionally, and a coset form carries it once its operand is
    known antisymmetric within each group. See
    `DESIGN-permutation-operator-folding.md`.

    **What the guard covers, and what it does not**

    The rewrite is justified by the tensors bound when the pass ran, so it emits an
    ``OpKind::Setup`` body that re-checks those facts and throws naming the
    tensor. A BIND clears that body's ``computed`` flag, so the check runs once per
    bound problem.

    An IN-PLACE mutation of a checked input is not a bind, and the graph cannot see
    it: the tensor belongs to the caller, no node of this graph writes it, and its
    address does not change. So a caller who overwrites the contents of a tensor
    this pass validated, and then replays without rebinding, gets the folded
    arithmetic against data nothing re-checked. That is the boundary, stated rather
    than papered over, and ``Graph::invalidate_setup`` is the way across it: it
    puts the guard back to work on the next execute.

    The shape this arises in is narrower than it sounds. A quantity the graph
    itself updates is written by some node, and ``AntisymmetryDetection`` only
    ever looks at tensors NOTHING writes, so an amplitude a captured iteration
    updates was never a premise in the first place.

    **Ordering**

    Before ``AntisymmetrizerExpansion``, which strips the operator and so removes
    the thing this matches on.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_candidates
        :type: int

        Contractions examined whose operand came from an operator.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_folded
        :type: int

        Contractions rewritten.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: AntisymmetrizerLinearity

    Pull a sum of antisymmetrized quantities inside one antisymmetrizer.

    A permutation operator is LINEAR, so

    .. math::

       a\,P[G](B) \;+\; s\,\bigl(c\,P[G](A)\bigr) \;=\; P[G]\bigl(a B + s c A\bigr)

    and a graph that antisymmetrizes two things and adds them can antisymmetrize
    once instead.

    **Why it matters more than the node it saves**

    On its own the rewrite trades one permuted-accumulation sweep for an add, which
    is worth having and is not the point. The point is that the sum's producer
    becomes an OPERATOR again. ``AntisymmetrizerFolding`` matches a contraction
    whose operand was written by an operator, and a residual writes
    `V := W + P(Xd)`, so the operand's producer is a linear combination and the
    fold cannot see the operator standing behind it. This puts it back where the
    fold can reach, which is what turns an N-term antisymmetrizer into a scalar
    multiply.

    It is the same move the hand-written folded spelling of
    `examples/toy/ccsd_t_spinorbital_toy.py` makes when it accumulates
    `Xc := Xc + Xd` and antisymmetrizes once.

    **What it matches**

    - a tensor written exactly twice: an operator with a ZERO destination prefactor, then an `Axpby` accumulating another tensor into it;
    - that other tensor written exactly once, by an operator with the SAME groups and a zero destination prefactor;
    - both tensors graph-owned intermediates untouched by a child sub-graph.

    The first operator's source is replaced by a freshly built sum, its prefactor
    folded into that sum, and the accumulation deleted. The second operator is left
    standing, because a residual reads it separately (the toy divides it by the
    energy denominator) and ``DeadNodeElimination`` removes it when nothing does.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_candidates
        :type: int

        Sites where one antisymmetrized quantity accumulates into another.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_merged
        :type: int

        Sites rewritten.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: AntisymmetryDetection

    Establish, by reading the data, which of a permutation operator's symmetries a bound input actually has.

    ``AntisymmetryInference`` proves what an operator's output has STRUCTURALLY,
    which reaches only the singleton-partition case. The facts a coupled-cluster
    residual needs are about the problem rather than the graph: that the
    amplitudes are antisymmetric in a pair of slots, that an energy denominator is
    invariant under the permutations the residual applies. Nothing derives those,
    and this pass does not ask a user to assert them either. It looks.

    For each operator in the graph it forms two candidate descriptors over the
    operator's output axes, and tests them against the bound inputs of matching
    shape:

    - **within-group antisymmetry**, which is the precondition a coset operator's output needs before it carries any antisymmetry of its own;
    - **invariance** under each permutation the operator sums over, which is what lets an elementwise division by that tensor preserve a numerator's antisymmetry.

    Whatever holds is recorded on ``TensorHandle::symmetry_hint``, which is graph
    metadata. The backing tensor is never written, so a user's declared symmetry,
    or absence of one, is left exactly as it was.

    **Only inputs, and why that is not a limitation but a correctness gate**

    A tensor written by some node in this graph holds, at optimize time, whatever
    it was initialized to. Graph-owned scratch is typically ZERO, and a zero tensor
    satisfies every symmetry there is. Tagging one would record a fact that is true
    of the buffer now and false of the value the graph will put in it, which is the
    most direct route to a wrong answer this design has. So a candidate must have
    NO writer in the graph: it is a bound input, and what is in it at optimize time
    is what the contraction will read.

    **Cost**

    One pass over a candidate per generator that HOLDS, and effectively nothing per
    generator that does not, since ``check_symmetry`` stops at the first
    violation: measured at 1.47 ns an element against 0.4 microseconds for a whole
    rank-6 tensor whose generator fails. Probing many candidates is therefore cheap
    and only a real symmetry costs a sweep.

    **The premise is DATA, which constrains what may be built on it**

    A fact established here describes the tensors bound right now. A rebind
    replaces them, and `ContractionPlanning` states the house rule this runs into:
    a measurement may found a hint, not a premise. Anything that REWRITES
    arithmetic on the strength of one of these facts therefore has to be re-checked
    per bound problem, which is what ``OpKind::Setup`` exists for. See
    `DESIGN-permutation-operator-folding.md`.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_probed
        :type: int

        (tensor, generator) pairs tested against the data.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_found
        :type: int

        Generators found to hold.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_tensors
        :type: int

        Tensors that gained at least one generator.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: AntisymmetryInference

    Tag the output of a permutation operator with the antisymmetry it unconditionally has.

    A node whose spec names a permutation operator (``PermutationOperator``)
    produces a tensor with structure, and this records what that structure is so a
    later pass can rewrite arithmetic against it. Today the consumer is
    ``AntisymmetrizerFolding``.

    **The rule, and the narrow case it covers**

    For an operator whose groups are all SINGLETONS, the expansion is
    :math:`P = \sum_\sigma \mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\,\sigma` over the whole symmetric
    group on its letters, so :math:`\tau P = \mathrm{sign}(\tau) P` for every
    transposition and the output is antisymmetric in those axes whatever the
    operand was.

    For an operator with a group of two or more this is FALSE, and measurably so.
    `P(i/jk)` applied to arbitrary rank-3 data is antisymmetric under none of
    `(ij)`, `(ik)` or `(jk)`: a coset form carries the antisymmetry only when its
    operand already has it within each group, which is the same precondition the
    operator's well-definedness rests on. Those operators are DECLINED here rather
    than tagged, and reaching them needs a fact about the operand that this pass
    has no way to establish. See `DESIGN-permutation-operator-folding.md`.

    **Soundness**

    Three gates, all load-bearing:

    - the destination prefactor must be ZERO, since with a nonzero one the result is the previous contents plus an antisymmetric part, which is not antisymmetric;
    - the tensor must have exactly one writer in this graph and no reference from a child sub-graph, which is ``EscapeAnalysis``, shared with ``SymmetryPropagation`` rather than re-derived;
    - only graph-owned intermediates are tagged, never a user's tensor.

    **Ordering**

    Before ``AntisymmetrizerExpansion``, which strips the operator and so destroys
    the evidence this rule reads. That is also why this is a separate pass from
    ``SymmetryPropagation``, which runs after Materialization for a different
    purpose and would find nothing left to infer from.

    Writes only ``TensorHandle::symmetry_hint``, which is graph metadata, so it
    needs no backing storage and can run before anything is materialized.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_candidates
        :type: int

        WRITES a rule examined, across every kind the rules cover. A plain
        contraction counts: R3 asks of every einsum whether its operands carry an
        antisymmetry it could pass on, and asking is what makes it a candidate.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_tagged
        :type: int

        TENSORS tagged antisymmetric. A tensor rather than a write, because a
        tensor built by accumulation is settled by all of its writes together.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: ApproximationRecord

    One lossy rewrite that has been applied to a graph, and what it cost in accuracy.

    Accumulated on the ``Graph`` and SAVED with its structure, because it is a statement about
    what the graph now computes rather than about how it runs: a caller reading a result off a
    loaded graph is entitled to know that the energy is DF at 1e-5, and no machine can recover
    that from the node list.

    It is three things at once. Provenance for the user, the input to the composition rule that
    lets a second lossy pass decide whether it may apply, and the trigger that widens a
    differential test's tolerance from exact to whatever the records say.

    .. seealso::

       Graph::note_approximation

    .. seealso::

       Graph::approximation_tolerance

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:attribute:: pass_name
        :type: str

        The pass that applied the rewrite, by ``OptimizerPass::name``.

        Not spelled ``pass``, which is what it wants to be called and is a Python keyword:
        the field would bind to a name no Python source can write down. The saved key matches
        this spelling rather than the shorter one, so the file and the type do not need a
        translation nobody would remember was there.

    .. py:attribute:: tolerance
        :type: float

        The knob the pass was ASKED for, e.g. a ``thc_epsilon`` of 1e-5. Recorded beside
        ``bound`` rather than instead of it: a pass may well meet its target with room to
        spare, and a composition rule that read the target would spend accuracy nobody used.

    .. py:attribute:: effect
        :type: ApproximationEffect

        What ``bound`` is a bound on.

    .. py:attribute:: bound
        :type: float

        The effect the pass STATES it had, in ``effect``'s units. This is what composes and
        what a tolerance-aware comparison widens by.

    .. py:attribute:: origin
        :type: ApproximationOrigin

        Whether ``bound`` was computed or claimed. Defaults to ``ApproximationOrigin::Asserted``,
        which is also how a file written before this field existed is read: a number cannot be
        promoted to evidence by a newer build reading it.

    .. py:attribute:: outputs
        :type: list[str]

        Manifest names the bound applies to. EMPTY means every output, which is the honest
        answer for a pass that rewrote something feeding all of them.

    .. py:attribute:: spaces
        :type: list[str]

        Index-space names the rewrite involved, by NAME for the reason every other saved
        annotation holds names: an id means nothing in another process.

    .. py:attribute:: setup
        :type: str

        Label of the setup node the rewrite created, or empty when it created none. A
        factorization emits its fitting into one, and the record is where the two are tied
        together for anyone asking what produced these factors.

    .. py:attribute:: measurement
        :type: str

        The TENSOR whose value after a replay is this rewrite's MEASURED error, by name, or
        empty when the rewrite measures nothing.

        A record is written once, at optimize time, so ``bound`` is what the structure claims
        and cannot be what a particular bind found. What a bind can leave behind is a value in
        the graph, and this is where the record says which one, so a caller holding a record does
        not have to know how the pass spells its diagnostics.

        A tensor rather than a parameter, and that is a correction rather than a preference: the
        ``ParamTable`` is integer-valued, so a residual below one truncates to zero there and a
        number that cannot represent what it measures is not a measurement. The precedent that
        works is the error tensor a Laplace quadrature already writes.

        It is also the whole of the rule for a rewrite INSIDE A LOOP. A fit re-fitted at every
        amplitude update writes this tensor once per iteration, so what stands in it when the
        solver stops is the fit's error at the LAST iteration, which is the iteration the
        converged answer came out of. There is no per-iteration history and there should not be:
        the intermediate fits are error in a quantity nobody kept.


.. py:class:: ApproximationTolerance

    The comparison tolerance a set of approximation records implies.

    Two numbers rather than one because the effects do not share units: an absolute bound
    widens the absolute side of a comparison and a relative one widens the relative side.
    Which is which is ``is_absolute_effect``, in one place.

    Both zero means the graph carries no records, which is exact mode and today's behavior.

    .. seealso::

       Graph::approximation_tolerance

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:attribute:: relative
        :type: float

        Composed bound of every relative record. Widens a comparison's relative tolerance.

    .. py:attribute:: absolute
        :type: float

        Composed bound of every absolute record. Widens a comparison's absolute tolerance.


.. py:class:: AxisTiling

    Rewrite a program as a loop over slices of its free axes, under a memory cap.

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::PassManager manager;
         auto tiling = std::make_shared<cg::passes::AxisTiling>();
         tiling->set_memory_cap(4096);
         manager.add(tiling);
         graph.apply(manager);

    A member of ``PassManager::create_default``, after the algebraic rewrites. By default the cap
    is what the ``--einsums:max-memory`` option leaves after every other tensor of the graph, so in the default
    pipeline the pass streams only a program that would not fit; on one that fits it does no more
    than run a provably symmetric region as a triangle where that is cheaper. A caller with a smaller footprint to live inside sets a cap of
    its own.

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

        Default-construct, reading the cap from ``option::GraphTilingMemoryCap``.


    .. py:method:: set_memory_cap(bytes: int) -> None

        Override the cap for this pipeline, in bytes.

        Beats ``option::GraphTilingMemoryCap``, which is the two-level shape every other
        per-pass knob in this module has.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param bytes: Largest a single live intermediate may be. Zero disables the pass, and a negative value derives the cap from the ``--einsums:max-memory`` option and the graph's other tensors.


    .. py:method:: set_block_extent(extent: int) -> None

        Pin the block extent of a triangle's two axes.

        A triangle's block is priced from the machine's measured kernel rates, so the block it
        chooses moves between machines. A test that checks the emission at a given block pins it
        here. A pinned block that does not fit the memory the region may take declines the triangle.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param extent: The block, two or more; zero lets the cost model choose, which is the default.


    .. py:method:: use_reference_cost_model() -> None

        Price against the cost model's built-in rates instead of the detected machine's.

        The pass prices each candidate from the machine's measured kernel rates, so where two
        candidates are close the one it chooses moves between machines. With the built-in rates the
        decision is the same on every machine, which is what a test that checks the choice needs.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:attribute:: name
        :type: str

        See ``OptimizerPass::name``.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: memory_cap
        :type: int

        The cap in force: this pipeline's override, or the option's value.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_tiled
        :type: int

        How many regions the last apply tiled.

        The other getters describe the first of them, the region around the largest intermediate;
        ``explain`` names every one.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: axis_names
        :type: list[str]

        The sliced axes of the last decision, named ``tensor[position]``.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: axis_letters
        :type: list[str]

        The index letter each sliced axis carries, where a contraction names one.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: axis_extents
        :type: list[int]

        The extent of each sliced axis.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: slice_count
        :type: int

        How many slices the chosen axes cut the program into.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: depth
        :type: int

        How many slices one iteration of the emitted loop handles.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: iterations
        :type: int

        How many iterations the emitted loops run.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: largest_before
        :type: int

        The largest single intermediate any examined graph declared before the rewrite, in bytes.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: largest_after
        :type: int

        The largest single intermediate the body declares after the rewrite, in bytes.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: streamed
        :type: list[str]

        The intermediates the rewrite streams, by name.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: whole
        :type: list[str]

        The tensors the rewrite left whole, by name.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: emission
        :type: str

        How the last decision emits a chunk.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: block_extents
        :type: list[int]

        The block extent of each sliced axis.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: loops
        :type: int

        How many loops the rewrite emitted.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: triangle_sign
        :type: int

        The sign a triangle's mirrored tiles carry.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: accumulator
        :type: str

        The tensor the reduction accumulates into across iterations, if there is one.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: summed_into
        :type: list[str]
