    .. py:method:: for_tag(tag: str) -> list[FactorizationProvider]

        Every provider claiming ``tag``, in registration order.

        :param tag: A provenance tag name.
        :returns: The providers. Empty when nothing claims it.


    .. py:method:: claims(tag: str) -> bool

        Whether any provider claims ``tag``.

        :param tag: A provenance tag name.
        :returns: True when at least one does.


    .. py:method:: remove(name: str) -> bool

        Remove a provider by name.

        :param name: The provider's ``FactorizationProvider::name``.
        :returns: True when one was removed.


    .. py:method:: clear() -> None

        Forget every provider.


    .. py:attribute:: size
        :type: int

        How many providers are registered.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: GateFlags

    An array of conditional gates a graph reads without calling back into its caller.

    ``Graph::add_conditional`` takes a ``std::function<bool()>``, which is the general form and the
    right one when the answer has to be computed. It is the wrong one when the answer is already
    known before the replay starts and the graph holds hundreds of conditionals: a predicate bound to
    a Python callable takes the GIL on every evaluation, so a graph whose conditionals are executed
    from several threads serializes on it, and a caller that already knows every answer pays that
    serialization for nothing.

    This is the other form. The caller writes the whole array once per replay, through ``fill`` or
    ``assign``, and each conditional reads one slot of it. The buffer is shared: @ref
    Graph::add_conditional_flag bakes a copy of the ``shared_ptr`` into the node at capture time, so
    the flags survive however long the graph does even if the handle the caller held is dropped
    first, and every node sees the same array on every replay. Evaluation is a load and a compare.

    The flags ride outside the dataflow and carry no ordering of their own, exactly as ``LuPivots``
    does. Nothing serializes a write to a flag against a replay that reads it; the caller must finish
    writing before it calls ``execute()``.

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::GateFlags live(blocks.size(), true);
       for (size_t b = 0; b < blocks.size(); b++) {
           auto [branch, _] = graph.add_conditional_flag(fmt::format("block [{}]", b), live, b);
           CaptureGuard const guard(branch);
           emit(blocks[b]);
       }
       live.set(3, false);   // block 3 is skipped by the next replay
       graph.execute();


    .. py:method:: __init__(count: int = 0, value: bool = False) -> None

        ``count`` flags, all ``value``.


    .. py:method:: resize(count: int, value: bool = False) -> None

        Grow or shrink the array, giving any new flag ``value``.

        Resizing while a graph holds indices into the array is the caller's problem: an index past
        the end reads false, which silently skips a branch rather than reading out of bounds.


    .. py:method:: get(index: int) -> bool

        The flag at ``index``.


    .. py:method:: set(index: int, value: bool) -> None

        Set the flag at ``index``.


    .. py:method:: fill(value: bool) -> None

        Set every flag to ``value``.


    .. py:method:: assign(values: list[int]) -> None

        Replace the whole array in one call, which is the point of the type: a caller that knows
        every answer writes them all at once instead of once per conditional.


    .. py:attribute:: size
        :type: int

        How many flags the array holds.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: Graph

    A directed acyclic graph (DAG) of tensor operations.

    The Graph class is the central container for the computation graph. It stores
    a sequence of operation nodes and the tensor handles they reference. Key features:

    - **Capture**: Operations are added during a capture phase (via CaptureGuard).
    - **Topological sorting**: Nodes are sorted based on data dependencies before execution.
    - **Execution**: All nodes execute in dependency order. Calling execute() multiple times replays the same operations (useful for iterative algorithms).
    - **Optimization**: Passes can be applied to fuse, eliminate, or reorder nodes.
    - **Tensor ownership**: Intermediate tensors can be created via create_tensor() so the graph manages their lifetimes.
    - **Validation**: Before execution, tensor pointers are checked for use-after-free.
    - **Profiling**: execute() wraps each node in profiler regions with annotations.
    - **Visualization**: print_dot() exports GraphViz format; print_summary() prints text.

    .. code-block:: text

       namespace cg = einsums::compute_graph;

       auto A = create_random_tensor<double>("A", 10, 5);
       auto B = create_random_tensor<double>("B", 5, 8);

       cg::Graph graph("my_graph");

       // declare_, not create_: C is a RESULT this code reads after execute(), so it must not be
       // marked scratch. create_zero_tensor would make it an intermediate, and DeadNodeElimination
       // removes the einsum below the moment the graph is optimized, leaving C as it was.
       auto &C = graph.declare_zero_tensor<double, 2>("C", 10, 8);

       {
           cg::CaptureGuard guard(graph);
           cg::einsum("ik;kj->ij", &C, A, B);
       }

       graph.optimize();  // also materializes C, whose allocation declare_ deferred

       graph.execute();   // C = A * B

       graph.execute();   // Replay: C = A * B again

    .. seealso::

       CaptureGuard for the RAII capture mechanism

    .. seealso::

       Pipeline for multi-stage workflows with loops

    .. seealso::

       OptimizerPass for graph optimization


    .. py:method:: __init__(name: str = "graph") -> None

        Construct an empty graph.

        :param name: Human-readable name for profiling and debugging output.


    .. py:method:: verify() -> list[str]

        Every structural invariant this graph and its sub-graphs break, one message each.

        Checks that every node id is issued and unique within its graph, that every operand is a
        registered tensor, that each lifecycle node names the registered tensor it manages, that
        every node has an executor, and that each contraction and permutation carries index lists
        as long as its operands' ranks. Backings are checked both ways: no node may write a tensor
        whose backing is only read, and a tensor with a write backing must be written, in memory or
        straight into its storage. Empty for a well-formed graph. Run after every pass when
        ``einsums:pass:verify`` is set.


    .. py:method:: link_alias_storage() -> None

        Link a freshly registered handle to any registered tensor whose storage contains it (or that it contains).

        ``cg::view()`` sets ``TensorHandle::aliases`` itself, but a view sliced
        OUTSIDE a capture never goes through it: Python's capture-aware
        ``__getitem__`` falls through to the eager slice, and the resulting view
        reaches the graph as an ordinary operand on first use. Registered as-is
        it has ``aliases == 0``, so the scheduler sees no relationship to its
        parent and is free to order a read of the parent before a write through
        the view. That produced silently wrong results with no error, and the
        pattern is not exotic: a captured view must outlive the graph, which
        pushes callers to build views up front.

        So containment is detected here from the registration-time data pointer
        and strides, which closes the hole whatever the view's provenance.
        Runs once over all registered tensors rather than per registration:
        a containment test is O(n) against what is already there, so doing it on
        every registration is quadratic, and a DLPNO-MP2 capture registers ~13k
        tensors. Idempotent and cheap to call; register_tensor just marks it
        stale.

        .. note::

           DELEGATES to ``link_alias_structural`` when no handle in the graph
           carries an address at all, which is the state of a graph read from a
           file: nothing is allocated, so there is nothing to compare. Linking
           nothing there is not a safe default - it is the shape of the
           full-cover alias bug, a silently incomplete relation that surfaces
           as a race rather than an error.

        .. note::

           A MIXED graph - some handles with addresses, some without - is the
           ordinary case, not an error: a deferred shell has no address until
           it is materialized, and ``TensorHandle::data_ptr`` is a
           registration-time snapshot nothing refreshes. The rule is that the
           pointer derivation runs whenever ANY handle carries an address and
           simply cannot see a null-address handle, unchanged from before,
           while the structural derivation takes the whole graph only when NONE
           does. Spanning both modes are ``declare_alias`` declarations, which
           are applied first in either and are the only way an alias between
           two address-less operands is expressible at all.

        .. seealso::

           link_alias_structural


    .. py:method:: link_alias_structural() -> None

        Derive the alias relation from ``View`` nodes and manifest declarations, with no address consulted anywhere.

        The counterpart of ``link_alias_storage`` for a graph that has no
        addresses to derive from, and the mechanism a loaded graph uses: the
        structure was read from a file, nothing is allocated, and the tensors
        bound afterwards are not the tensors that were captured. Writes its result
        through the same two fields - ``TensorHandle::aliases`` and
        ``TensorHandle::alias_box`` - so ``resolve_alias``, the hazard edges and
        ``rebind``'s redirect chain consume it unchanged.

        Two sources, and they are exhaustive because nothing else can survive a
        save:

        - **``View`` nodes.** A ``ViewDescriptor`` says which parent axis each result axis reads (through ``ViewDescriptor::permutation``) and what interval that axis is restricted to. Chains COMPOSE: a view of a view is placed inside its parent's own region and linked to the root, the same path compression the pointer derivation does and for the same reason - ``resolve_alias`` walks the chain on every hazard-scan lookup. A bound that is not a compile-time constant (a Param or a Callback, unknown until execute) yields NO box, so the view conflicts as its whole parent, which is the same conservative answer the pointer path gives for a region it cannot prove.
        - **Manifest declarations.** Two caller-supplied tensors that share storage with no ``View`` node recording it are declared through ``declare_alias``, which ``ManifestEntry::aliases_input`` carries across a save. The declaration names a buffer, never a region, so such a pair conflicts as the whole parent until the schema records boxes.

        Recurses into ``Loop`` bodies and ``Conditional`` branches, which are
        separate graphs with their own handles; a body left unlinked answers "this
        view aliases nothing" and loses its hazard edges silently.

        .. note::

           Does NOT mark the pointer derivation as done. Running this and then
           ``link_alias_storage`` on a graph with addresses leaves the relation
           unchanged, which is the equivalence stated the other way round and is
           what every scheduling path enforces once this has been called.

        .. note::

           Idempotent, and safe to run on a graph that HAS addresses - it is
           then strictly a second opinion, describing the ``View`` chains the
           pointer path skipped (it skips any handle capture already linked) and
           leaving the pointer-only relations, a reshaped window of a scratch
           pool among them, to ``link_alias_storage``. Structural is therefore
           not a replacement for the pointer derivation on a live graph; it is
           what a graph with no addresses has instead of one.

        .. seealso::

           link_alias_storage

        .. seealso::

           declare_alias

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: clear_alias_links() -> None

        Forget every derived alias link, keeping the declared ones.

        Clears ``TensorHandle::aliases`` and ``TensorHandle::alias_box`` on every
        handle and marks the relation stale, so the next ``link_alias_storage``
        rebuilds it from scratch. Exists so the two derivations can be compared on
        the same footing - the equivalence is tested rather than hoped for - and so
        a loader can install a fresh relation over a graph that
        carries a stale one.

        .. note::

           Declarations made through ``declare_alias`` SURVIVE, because they
           are an input to the derivation rather than an output of it.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: set_space_registry(registry: SpaceRegistry) -> None

        Resolve this graph's index spaces against ``registry`` instead of the global one.

        Set it BEFORE annotating anything. Ids already stored on this graph's handles were issued
        by the previous registry and are not translated.

        :param registry: The registry to use. It must outlive the graph.


    .. py:method:: annotate_spaces(id: int, spaces: list[SpaceId]) -> None
                   annotate_spaces(tensor: Any, spaces: list[SpaceId]) -> None

        Annotate a registered tensor's axes with the index spaces they range over.

        Annotation is a declaration, so it is checked here, once, rather than on any path that
        later reads it. Annotate before capturing the operations that use the tensor: capture
        reads the annotation off the handle to bind the letters of each contraction, and an
        annotation added afterwards does not reach nodes that are already recorded.

        :param id: The tensor to annotate.
        :param spaces: One space per axis, in axis order. Pass an empty vector to clear the annotation. Every axis must name a real space; to leave one axis unannotated, see ``annotate_space_axis``.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.
        :raises Exception: if the count differs from the tensor's rank, or if any id is invalid or does not resolve in ``space_registry``.


    .. py:method:: annotate_space_axis(id: int, axis: int, space: SpaceId) -> None

        Annotate ONE axis, leaving the others as they are.

        ``annotate_spaces`` takes THE COMPLETE annotation and rightly refuses a hole in it: a
        caller handing over a whole vector has said something about every axis, and an invalid id
        in one slot is far more likely a mistake than an intention. This is how the hole is made
        deliberately, one axis at a time, for a tensor that mixes axes over a space with axes
        whose extent is a fixed number meaning nothing chemically - a DIIS history depth beside a
        pair of orbital indices. Axes nobody has named stay unannotated, which every reader of
        the annotation already handles per axis.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param id: The tensor to annotate.
        :param axis: Which axis.
        :param space: The space that axis ranges over.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.
        :raises Exception: if ``axis`` is past the tensor's rank, or if ``space`` is invalid or does not resolve in ``space_registry``.


    .. py:method:: tensor_spaces(id: int) -> list[SpaceId]
                   tensor_spaces(tensor: Any) -> list[SpaceId]

        The index spaces annotated on a registered tensor's axes.

        :param id: The tensor to read.
        :returns: One space per axis, or an empty vector when the tensor carries no annotation.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.


    .. py:method:: annotate_tag(id: int, tag: ProvenanceTag) -> None
                   annotate_tag(tensor: Any, tag: ProvenanceTag) -> None

        Declare what a tensor IS, by name.

        Index spaces say how big a tensor's axes are; this says what the tensor is, which is the
        question a pass that wants to RECOGNIZE something has to ask. ``DeltaElimination`` looks
        for ``provenance_identity``; a factorization provider will look for its own name.

        The vocabulary is open and unvalidated on purpose, because the set of things worth
        recognizing grows with the passes that recognize them. The cost is real and worth stating:
        a misspelled name is a tag nothing matches, and the only symptom is a pass that quietly
        finds no candidates. A pass is expected to report that through its skip tally rather than
        to stay silent.

        Attributes are sorted on the way in, so two tags built by setting the same keys in a
        different order compare equal and a saved graph's bytes do not depend on call order.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param id: The tensor to tag.
        :param tag: The tag. An empty name clears any tag the tensor carried.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.


    .. py:method:: tensor_tag(id: int) -> ProvenanceTag
                   tensor_tag(tensor: Any) -> ProvenanceTag

        What a registered tensor is declared to be.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param id: The tensor to read.
        :returns: The tag, whose ``ProvenanceTag::name`` is empty for an untagged tensor.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.


    .. py:method:: annotate_backing(tensor: Any, backing: TensorBacking) -> None

        Declare a tensor's backing, addressing it by the caller's tensor object.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param tensor: The tensor, declared on this graph and still deferred.
        :param backing: The storage.


    .. py:method:: tensor_backing(tensor: Any) -> TensorBacking | None

        The storage a tensor is backed by, addressing it by the caller's object.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param tensor: The tensor to read.
        :returns: The backing, or nothing.
        :raises Exception: if this graph has never seen that tensor.


    .. py:method:: space_extent(space: SpaceId) -> int | None

        The extent this graph has learned for ``space``, if it knows one.

        Learned, not declared: ``annotate_spaces`` on a tensor whose dims are concrete says how
        big that space is on this problem, so the graph records it rather than making the caller
        repeat it. Two annotated axes over one space that disagree leave the space UNPINNED, which
        is not an error - a PNO domain legitimately has a different extent per pair - but it does
        mean a space-typed dimension cannot be sized from it.

        .. seealso::

           declare_zero_runtime_tensor(std::string, std::vector<SpaceDim>, bool)

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param space: The space to ask about.
        :returns: The extent, or an empty optional when no annotated tensor has pinned it or when two of them disagree.


    .. py:method:: space_tiling(space: SpaceId) -> list[int] | None

        How ``space`` is canonically cut up, if this graph has been told.

        A tiled program usually cuts one space the same way everywhere - every occupied axis in a
        tiled CC lands on the same block boundaries - so the partition is worth saying ONCE, at
        the space, instead of at every tensor that has such an axis. Retiling is then one edit
        rather than one per declaration.

        Unlike ``space_extent`` this is never learned: a tensor's tiling is a property of that
        tensor, and taking the first one seen as canonical would silently make one scratch
        buffer's layout everyone else's.

        .. seealso::

           pin_space_tiling

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param space: The space to ask about.
        :returns: The tile sizes, or an empty optional when none was stated or two statements disagreed.


    .. py:method:: pin_space_tiling(space: SpaceId, tile_sizes: list[int]) -> None

        State how ``space`` is cut up, for every tiled axis that ranges over it.

        Also pins the extent to the sum, because a partition states one: a space cut into
        ``{2,2}`` measures 4, and leaving those two facts able to disagree would be a bug waiting
        to happen.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param space: The space.
        :param tile_sizes: The partition. Empty clears it.
        :raises Exception: If ``space`` is invalid, if any tile is not positive, or if the tiles sum to something other than an extent already known for ``space``.


    .. py:method:: pin_space_extent(space: SpaceId, extent: int) -> None

        State what ``space`` measures on this problem, rather than waiting to learn it.

        For the graph whose first tensor over a space is the one being declared, so there is
        nothing to have learned from yet. A later disagreeing annotation still unpins the space.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param space: The space.
        :param extent: Its extent. Zero clears whatever was known.


    .. py:method:: annotate_dims(id: int, symbols: list[str]) -> None
                   annotate_dims(tensor: Any, symbols: list[str]) -> None

        Declare which of a tensor's extents a ``bind`` may move, and which move together.

        The extent counterpart of ``annotate_spaces``, and what makes a captured graph valid
        for a family of problem sizes rather than for the one geometry it was captured at.
        ``rebind`` rejects any dim mismatch, which is right for a same-problem pointer swap
        and fatal for cross-problem reuse; a symbol is how a caller says "this axis is
        ``nv``, whatever ``nv`` turns out to be next time".

        **Symbol-space ties**

        When an annotated axis also carries a ``annotate_spaces`` annotation, the pair
        ``(symbol, space)`` is recorded. One symbol tied to two different spaces anywhere in
        one graph is a contradiction - ``nv`` cannot be both the virtual space and the
        auxiliary basis - and is refused here, where both spaces are still in hand, rather
        than surfacing as an unsolvable bind. The tie is recorded from either side, so
        annotating spaces after dims catches it too.

        .. seealso::

           bind

        .. seealso::

           symbol_spaces

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param id: The tensor to annotate.
        :param symbols: One entry per axis, in axis order. The empty string leaves an axis LITERAL (a bind must match it exactly). Any other string names a symbol: every axis in this graph carrying that symbol has one extent, and a bind solves it. A ``"ragged:<space>"`` spelling (see ``make_ragged_symbol``, or use ``annotate_ragged_dim``) marks an axis whose extent differs per instance. Pass an empty vector to clear the annotation.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.
        :raises Exception: if the count differs from the tensor's rank; if a ragged symbol names a space ``space_registry`` cannot resolve; or if a symbol is already tied to a DIFFERENT index space elsewhere in this graph.


    .. py:method:: annotate_ragged_dim(id: int, axis: int, space_name: str) -> None
                   annotate_ragged_dim(tensor: Any, axis: int, space_name: str) -> None

        Declare one axis ragged over an index space.

        One symbol per space is not enough for the workload that motivates symbolic extents:
        a PNO domain has a different virtual extent per pair, and a block or tiled tensor
        carries per-block dims, so no single ``nv`` describes them. A ragged axis says so
        explicitly instead of pretending otherwise - it constrains nothing across slots, and
        a bind accepts whatever extent each bound instance carries.

        Sugar over ``annotate_dims``: it writes ``make_ragged_symbol`` into ``axis`` of the
        tensor's existing symbol vector, creating an all-literal one first if the tensor has
        none.

        .. seealso::

           bind_ragged_extents

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param id: The tensor to annotate.
        :param axis: Which axis is ragged.
        :param space_name: The index space the axis is ragged over.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.
        :raises Exception: if ``axis`` is past the tensor's rank, or if ``space_name`` does not resolve in ``space_registry``.


    .. py:method:: tensor_dim_symbols(id: int) -> list[str]
                   tensor_dim_symbols(tensor: Any) -> list[str]

        The symbolic extent declaration on a registered tensor's axes.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param id: The tensor to read.
        :returns: One entry per axis, or an empty vector when every axis is literal.
        :raises Exception: if no tensor with that id is registered.


    .. py:method:: execute() -> None
                   execute(executor: Executor) -> None

        Execute all nodes in topological order.

        Performs topological sorting (if not already sorted), validates that all
        tensor pointers are still alive, then executes each node's lambda in order.

        Can be called multiple times to replay the same computation sequence.
        All tensors must have the same dimensions as at capture time.

        :raises Exception: If a tensor appears to have been destroyed (use-after-free detected).
        :raises Exception: If a cycle is detected during topological sort.


    .. py:method:: set_executor(executor: Executor) -> None

        Choose the executor plain execute() uses for THIS graph.

        The stored executor applies wherever this graph is executed without an
        explicit executor argument - most importantly loop bodies, which are
        replayed by the loop node via the argument-less execute() and had no
        other way to run on a parallel backend:

        .. code-block:: py

           body = g.add_loop("iter", 100, cont)
           body.set_executor(cg.DataflowExecutor())   # replays overlap independent nodes

        Pass nullptr/None to restore the built-in sequential path. The custom
        executors dispatch every node's CPU lambda directly, so graphs with
        GPU-placed nodes should keep the default (it performs the device
        shadow bookkeeping; the executors do not).

        :param executor: Shared ownership of the executor, or nullptr to reset.


    .. py:method:: apply(pm: PassManager) -> bool

        Apply a PassManager (ordered sequence of passes) to the graph.

        Runs all passes in the PassManager in order.

        .. code-block:: text

           auto pm = cg::PassManager::create_default();
           graph.apply(pm);

        :param pm: The pass manager to run.
        :returns: True if any pass modified the graph.


    .. py:method:: optimize() -> bool
                   optimize(level: OptLevel) -> bool

        Optimize with the full default pipeline (OptLevel::O2).

        Compiler-style front door over PassManager: builds the pipeline, runs
        it, and records a human-readable summary retrievable via explain().

        .. code-block:: text

           graph.optimize();
           std::cout << graph.explain();


    .. py:method:: timing_report() -> list[NodeTiming]

        Access the timing data from the last execute() call. Labels are
        resolved here (cached until the next recorded sample), not on the
        replay path.

        :returns: One entry per node the last replay ran, in run order.


    .. py:method:: last_run() -> RunMetrics

        What the last completed replay did, measured as it ran.

        Assembled when asked for, from counts the replay kept: nodes add their times as every
        executor already records them, a backing counts its own reads and writes on whichever
        thread runs them, and lifecycle nodes count what they allocate and free.

        .. seealso::

           RunMetrics

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: Time per node (this graph's and every body's, a loop body's summed over its iterations), bytes and calls per backing, and the most graph-managed memory held at once. Empty before the first replay; a replay that threw leaves the one before.


    .. py:method:: resident_bytes() -> int

        The bytes a replay holds from its start to its end that no lifecycle node of it allocates or frees.

        The other half of the program's memory beside ``RunMetrics::peak_bytes``, which counts
        what the lifecycle nodes hold. Their sum is what the budget check compares with
        --einsums:max-memory.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The bytes of every tensor this graph and its bodies read or write that is in memory for the whole replay: the caller's tensors, and any intermediate given storage outside a Materialize node. Each tensor once; views and tensors a Materialize or Free node manages are left out. A backed tensor is read and written through buffers those nodes manage, so its stored bytes are not counted.


    .. py:method:: set_observer(observer: ExecutionObserver) -> None

        Attach ``observer`` to every replay of this graph and of every body under it.

        The observer belongs to the graph's tree, so attaching it to a body attaches it to the
        graph that holds the body too. Attach between replays, not during one.

        .. seealso::

           ExecutionObserver

        .. seealso::

           EventLog

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param observer: What hears the replays; null detaches the one attached.


    .. py:method:: to_dot() -> str

        The GraphViz text ``print_dot`` writes, as a string.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The DOT source.


    .. py:method:: summary() -> str

        The summary ``print_summary`` writes, as a string.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The summary text.


    .. py:method:: to_json() -> str

        Serialize the graph structure as a JSON string.

        Produces a JSON object with:

        - "name": graph name
        - "tensors": array of {id, name, rank, dims, element_size, dtype, is_intermediate}
        - "nodes": array of {id, kind, label, inputs, outputs, timing_ms}
        - "edges": array of {from_node, to_node, tensor_id} (data dependency edges)

        Used by the profile viewer to render an interactive node graph.
        Can be called at any point, edges are computed on-the-fly from node
        inputs/outputs, so this works both before and after execute().

        :returns: JSON string representation of the graph structure.


    .. py:method:: serializability_report() -> list[SerializabilityBlocker]

        The nodes that stand between this graph and being saved, with the reason for each.

        A graph can be written to a file only when every node can be rebuilt from
        ``(kind, dtype, rank, descriptor, operand ids)`` - see
        ``build_executor``. Conversion is per op kind and deliberately
        incremental, so "can this graph be saved" has an actionable answer rather
        than a boolean: this names each node that cannot, by id and label, and
        says what blocks it.

        .. note::

           Recursive: a ``Loop`` body and each ``Conditional`` branch is a
           separate sub-graph and is descended into, with
           ``SerializabilityBlocker::subgraph_path`` naming the path that
           reaches each blocker. The earlier flat version was justified by
           Loop and Conditional always blocking on their own closure-shaped
           predicates, which made a body unreachable-but-unreported an
           impossibility. Making both predicates data (``PredExpr``
           and ``BoundExpr``), so a clean control-flow node over a body full
           of closures now reports clean, and the justification is gone.

        .. seealso::

           reconstruction_blocker for the per-node verdict.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: One entry per blocking node, in node order, a control-flow node's own verdict immediately before its body's. Empty means the graph and everything below it is fully reconstructible.


    .. py:method:: content_hash() -> int

        A hash of this graph's STRUCTURE, and of nothing else.

        The digest of the canonical IR bytes with the provenance block removed:
        schema version, graph name, manifest, index spaces, parameters, gate-flag
        arrays, tensors, slot redirects and nodes. Two captures of the same
        program hash equal however they were produced and whoever wrote them; any
        structural difference - a changed prefactor, a reordered node, a renamed
        slot - changes the hash.

        .. note::

           Provenance-independent BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by convention:
           the hash is taken over the same object the writer emits, minus one
           key, so a field can only enter the hash domain by being put in the
           structure sections.

        .. seealso::

           save_graph

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The digest.
        :raises Exception: When the graph cannot be written at all (see ``serializability_report``), because a structure that has no canonical form has no hash either. The message carries the report.


    .. py:method:: num_nodes() -> int

    .. py:method:: num_tensors() -> int

    .. py:method:: schedule_edge_count() -> int

        How many hazard edges the schedule holds, and how wide its levels are.

        The two structural numbers a change to the dependence machinery moves:
        an access that cannot be described precisely widens to its whole buffer
        and picks up edges against every other access to it, and those edges are
        what cost a level scheduler its width. Both are deterministic, so they
        are what a test about ordering should assert - and what a measurement of
        ordering should report - rather than a wall clock or an energy.

        Both build the schedule if it is stale, exactly as ``execute()`` would,
        and so may reorder nodes into a topological order.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: ``schedule_edge_count`` the number of dependency edges; ``schedule_level_sizes`` the size of each level in order.


    .. py:method:: schedule_level_sizes() -> list[int]

        See ``schedule_edge_count``.


    .. py:method:: unjustified_hazard_edges() -> list[str]

        Hazard edges that nothing about the storage justifies.

        ``verify_level_independence`` asks whether an edge is MISSING, and every alias defect this
        module has shipped was found by it or by a race. The opposite direction has no detector at
        all, and it is the direction an over-conservative alias relation fails in: merging two
        unrelated tensors into one root costs a level scheduler its width and moves no number, so no
        differential oracle can see it. The deferred-parent merge cost every hazard between the two
        parents and was invisible for exactly that reason.

        The justification is derived WITHOUT consulting ``TensorHandle::aliases``, which is the
        relation under test. Two tensors may share memory when they are the same tensor, when one is
        reachable from the other through the graph's own ``View`` nodes, or when both have a known
        byte span and the spans overlap. A pair with none of those and no shared parameter name has
        no reason to be ordered.

        A tensor with no address is therefore related to another only structurally, which is what
        makes this see the deferred case: two deferred parents have no spans, so an edge between
        their writers can only come from an alias link that should not exist.

        Builds the schedule if it is stale, exactly as ``schedule_edge_count`` does.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: One line per unjustified edge, naming both nodes; empty when the edge set is minimal.


    .. py:method:: structural_passes() -> list[str]

        The structural-algebraic passes that have rewritten this graph, in order.

        Survives a save and a load, so a graph loaded from a file reports what shaped it before
        it was written rather than starting blank.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The names, empty on a graph nothing has rewritten.


    .. py:method:: machine_dependent_passes() -> list[str]

        The structural-resource and tuning passes that have changed this graph, in order.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The names, empty on a graph only capture and the structural passes have shaped.


    .. py:method:: approximations() -> list[ApproximationRecord]

        Every lossy rewrite applied to this graph, in the order it was applied.

        Saved with the structure and restored by a load, because a record says what the graph
        now computes rather than how it runs. Unlike ``structural_passes``, which is
        provenance and is read by nobody, these ARE acted on: a second lossy pass composes
        against them, and a tolerance-aware comparison widens by them.

        .. seealso::

           note_approximation

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The records, empty on a graph nothing has approximated. An empty list is EXACT mode: a differential comparison against eager stays at bit equality.


    .. py:method:: note_approximation(record: ApproximationRecord) -> None

        Record a lossy rewrite this graph has just had applied.

        A pass asks ``can_approximate`` BEFORE it rewrites anything, and declines through
        ``OptimizerPass::approximate`` when the answer is no.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param record: What the rewrite was and what it cost. See ``ApproximationRecord``.
        :raises Exception: With the reason, when ``can_approximate`` refuses it. Throwing rather than returning a flag, because a pass reaching here has already rewritten the graph: a refusal at this point is a pass that did not ask first, and the graph it leaves behind is wrong in a way no return value would fix.


    .. py:method:: can_approximate(candidate: ApproximationRecord) -> str

        Whether a pass may apply ``candidate``, and why not when it may not.

        Three ways the answer is no, and each is a real case rather than a guard against
        nonsense input:

        - The bound is negative or not finite. A pass that cannot state its effect has not met the contract, whatever else it can do.
        - The composed bound exceeds the declared budget for some output it names.
        - A budget is set and a record of a DIFFERENT effect already covers an output this one names. Only under a budget: bounds of different kinds coexist happily otherwise, and the tolerance carries them on separate sides. What a budget adds is a claim to be a cap, and a cap that governs one kind of error while another goes uncounted is misleading in a way that saying so is not.

        A graph with no budget therefore refuses only the first case: adding a lossy pass to a
        manager is the opt-in, and a budget is the further cap on what several may spend
        together.

        What is NOT decided here is whether a pass's own error model still holds once
        something else has perturbed its inputs. Only that pass knows; ``approximations`` is
        readable so it can look, and it declines through ``OptimizerPass::approximate`` like
        any other refusal.

        .. seealso::

           set_accuracy_budget

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param candidate: The rewrite a pass is about to apply.
        :returns: An empty string when it may; otherwise the reason, phrased for a skip tally.


    .. py:method:: accuracy_spent(effect: ApproximationEffect, output: str = "") -> float

        The composed bound already spent on one output.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param effect: The units to answer in. Records of other effects are not counted, because they do not convert.
        :param output: The manifest name to ask about, or empty for the graph-wide worst case, which counts every record whatever it names.
        :returns: The composed bound, 0 when nothing applies.


    .. py:method:: approximation_tolerance(output: str = "") -> ApproximationTolerance

        The comparison tolerance this graph's records imply for one output.

        The point of entry for Part 5.4's tolerance-aware differential mode, and the reason it
        lives here rather than in the harness: composition is arithmetic with a correctness
        argument behind it (see ``compose_approximation``), and a second implementation of it
        in the test layer would be a second thing to get right.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param output: The manifest name to ask about, or empty for the graph-wide worst case.
        :returns: The relative and absolute halves. Both zero on a graph with no records, which is exact mode.


    .. py:method:: set_accuracy_budget(effect: ApproximationEffect, value: float) -> None

        Cap what lossy passes may spend on this graph, together.

        Not set by default, and the default is not zero: a lossy pass runs only because a
        caller put it in a manager, and that is the opt-in. This is the second guard, for the
        caller who wants several of them and a ceiling on what they cost together.

        .. seealso::

           clear_accuracy_budget

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param effect: The units the cap is stated in. A pass bounding itself in any other effect will decline rather than convert.
        :param value: The largest composed bound any single output may carry.
        :raises Exception: If ``value`` is negative or not finite.


    .. py:method:: clear_accuracy_budget() -> None

        Remove the cap, if one was set.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: plan_threads(freeze: bool = False) -> bool

        Choose a thread width for every node, for this machine, now.

        Runs the ``passes::ThreadPlanning`` heuristic standalone - no pipeline
        needed, because widths touch no structure - and records the thread count
        the plan assumed so ``DataflowExecutor`` can notice a machine change and
        fall back to width 1. Loop bodies and conditional branches are planned
        too, each against the same thread count; the one process-wide width
        budget composes them at run time.

        Only ``DataflowExecutor`` acts on the result. Under the other executors
        the graph behaves exactly as an unplanned one.

        **Re-plan policy**

        A plan needs per-node serial times, and the honest ones are measured -
        but a measurement is taken under whatever plan and whatever concurrency
        produced it, so a plan computed FROM those timings and the plan they
        were measured UNDER are never comparable on paper: the re-plan's own
        estimate is polluted by the incumbent's schedule. (Concretely: co-run
        contention inflates every sample, the inflated area bound swallows the
        critical path, and a timings re-plan can then only ever narrow - it
        un-widened measurably profitable plans in every DLPNO trial.) The one
        comparator that settles it is the wall clock, so the re-plan is a
        measured trial rather than a decree:

        - A graph that has already replayed is planned from its recorded timings, and that plan is final.
        - A graph that has never replayed is planned from the cost model. Replay 1 warms up and records timings, and a re-plan is computed from them; if it chooses the same widths the trial ends there (keeping the re-plan's measured admission priorities). Otherwise replay 2 runs the re-planned widths, replay 3 runs the cold widths again - both steady-state - and the graph keeps whichever replay was faster, the incumbent unless the candidate clearly beat it. A trial the workload never finishes (fewer than three replays) leaves the cold plan in place.

        The consequence is deliberate and is the determinism contract: widths
        move only inside the trial window, so from the fourth replay onward -
        or from the first, with ``freeze`` - repeated replays at a fixed thread
        count are bit-identical.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param freeze: Plan now and never re-plan, even from the cold-start model. For workflows that want every replay bit-identical to the first, at the cost of model-quality widths.
        :returns: True when at least one node was given a width above 1.


    .. py:method:: add_conditional(label: str, predicate: Callable[[Any], bool]) -> tuple[Graph, Graph]

        Add a conditional (if-then-else) node to the graph.

        Returns references to the then-branch and else-branch subgraphs.
        Use CaptureGuard on each to capture operations into the branches.
        The predicate is evaluated at execution time to select the branch.

        .. code-block:: text

           auto [then_g, else_g] = graph.add_conditional("converge_check",
               [&]() { return delta < threshold; });
           { CaptureGuard g(then_g); cg::scale(1.0, &result); }
           { CaptureGuard g(else_g); cg::einsum(...); }

        :param label: Human-readable label for profiling.
        :param predicate: Function returning true for then-branch, false for else-branch. Can inspect tensor values and external state.
        :returns: Tuple of references: (then_branch_graph, else_branch_graph). A tuple (not a pair) so the method can be bound to Python, pybind11 can't cast a pair/tuple of *references* to a non-copyable type cleanly, but a reference-tuple return casts each branch with ``reference_internal``. Structured bindings (``auto [t, e] = ...``) work identically for C++ callers.


    .. py:method:: add_conditional_flag(label: str, flags: GateFlags, index: int) -> tuple[Graph, Graph]

        Add a conditional whose predicate is one flag of a ``GateFlags`` array.

        The same node ``add_conditional`` builds, with the predicate replaced by a load from a
        shared buffer. Use it when the answers are known before the replay starts and there are many
        of them: a ``std::function`` bound to a Python callable takes the GIL every time it is
        evaluated, and a graph whose conditionals run on several threads serializes on that.

        The array is shared, not copied, so the caller may keep writing to it between replays; a
        copy of the ``shared_ptr`` is baked into the node, so the flags outlive the handle. An index
        past the end of the array reads false.

        :param label: Human-readable label for profiling.
        :param flags: The array to read.
        :param index: Which flag in it selects this node's branch.
        :returns: Tuple of references: (then_branch_graph, else_branch_graph).


    .. py:method:: add_loop(label: str, max_iterations: int, condition: Callable[[int], bool]) -> Graph
                   add_loop(label: str, max_iterations: int, condition: Callable[[int], bool], body_fn: Callable[[Any], None]) -> None

        Add a loop node to the graph (Graph-returning variant).

        Returns a reference to the loop body subgraph. Use CaptureGuard to
        capture operations into the body.

        :param label: Human-readable label for profiling.
        :param max_iterations: Maximum number of iterations (safety limit).
        :param condition: Called after each iteration with the iteration number.
        :returns: Reference to the loop body Graph.


    .. py:method:: add_setup(label: str) -> Graph
                   add_setup(label: str, body_fn: Callable[[Any], None]) -> None

        Add a setup node: a body computed once per bound problem, not once per replay.

        Returns the body sub-graph, to be captured into with a ``CaptureGuard`` exactly as a
        loop body is. What goes in it is the computation that depends on WHAT is bound rather
        than on how many times the graph is replayed: a density fitting, a metric inverse, an
        integral transform. The replay body then reads its outputs and never recomputes them.

        Structurally this is a control-flow node like any other, which is the point: it takes
        its position in the ordinary schedule, its effective I/O is the sub-graph's reads and
        writes (so the scheduler orders it before every consumer with no special case), and a
        pass that walks sub-graphs reaches it through ``for_each_subgraph``. What is special
        is only the executor, which skips a body that has already computed.

        **When the body runs**

        At the first ``execute`` after capture, and at the first ``execute`` after any
        ``bind``. It is not run BY the bind: a bind that is never executed should cost
        nothing, and a caller who wants the factors on hand earlier calls ``run_setup``.

        **What a setup output must not be**

        Its outputs live across replays, so nothing may free them between two. @ref
        passes::FreeInsertion is told this rather than left to infer it from a live range that
        looks, within one replay, exactly like an ordinary intermediate's.

        .. code-block:: text

           auto &fit = graph.add_setup("df_fit");
           {
               cg::CaptureGuard const guard(fit);
               cg::einsum("Qmn <- QP ; Pmn", &B, metric_inv_sqrt, ints);
           }
           // ... the replay body reads B ...
           graph.execute();   // fits, then replays
           graph.execute();   // replays only

        .. seealso::

           run_setup

        .. seealso::

           set_setup_key

        .. seealso::

           invalidate_setup

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param label: Human-readable label for profiling and diagnostics.
        :returns: Reference to the setup body Graph. Valid for the life of this graph.


    .. py:method:: run_setup(force: bool = False) -> None

        Run every setup body that is not current, now.

        What the next ``execute`` would do anyway, pulled forward for a caller who wants the
        fitting cost paid at a moment of their choosing rather than inside the first replay's
        timing. Nothing else differs: the same guards apply, so a body already computed for
        the bound problem is skipped here exactly as it would be there.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param force: Run every body whatever its state, ignoring both the computed flag and the key. The escape hatch for a caller who changed the CONTENTS of a bound tensor without rebinding it, which nothing can observe.


    .. py:method:: invalidate_setup() -> None

        Mark every setup body as needing recomputation.

        Called by ``bind``, which is the one event that can change what a setup body would
        produce. Public because a caller who mutates a bound tensor in place has made the same
        change without going through a bind, and nothing else can see that.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: set_setup_key(key: str) -> None

        Name the problem currently bound, so a re-bind of it can skip refitting.

        The cache of the setup phase, and a claim the caller makes rather than one this graph
        checks. A bind invalidates every setup body; if the key set here is non-empty and equal
        to the key the body last computed for, the body is skipped anyway on the ground that
        the caller has said this is the same problem.

        Empty (the default) disables the cache: every bind refits, which is always correct.
        A caller supplying a key owns the consequence of supplying the same one for two
        different problems, which is a stale factorization and no diagnostic. Use something
        that identifies the problem rather than the call: a molecule and basis, a checkpoint
        id, a content hash the caller computed for its own reasons.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param key: The problem identity, or empty to disable the cache.


    .. py:method:: create_runtime_tensor(name: str, dims: list[int], intermediate: bool = True) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Create a graph-owned runtime-rank tensor.

        Runtime-rank analog of create_tensor(). Allocates a RuntimeTensor<T, Alloc>
        with rank determined by `dims.size()`, no rank-4 cap, no compile-time
        rank in the type. Adds an Alloc node and marks the handle as intermediate.
        Lives for the lifetime of the graph.

        .. code-block:: text

           auto &tmp = graph.create_runtime_tensor<double>("tmp", {100, 100});
           auto &big = graph.create_runtime_tensor<double>("big", {2, 3, 4, 5, 6});  // rank-5, fine


    .. py:method:: create_zero_runtime_tensor(name: str, dims: list[int], intermediate: bool = True) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor
                   create_zero_runtime_tensor(name: str, shape: list[SpaceDim], intermediate: bool = True) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Runtime-rank analog of create_zero_tensor().


    .. py:method:: declare_runtime_tensor(name: str, dims: list[int], intermediate: bool = False, backing: TensorBacking | None = None) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor
                   declare_runtime_tensor(name: str, shape: list[SpaceDim], intermediate: bool = False, backing: TensorBacking | None = None) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor
                   declare_runtime_tensor(name: str, backing: TensorBacking, intermediate: bool = False) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Declare a graph-owned runtime-rank tensor with DEFERRED allocation.

        The runtime-rank, pybind-exposed analog of declare_tensor(): a shell tensor
        (valid metadata, no data) registered in THIS graph's tensor map with
        AllocState::Deferred. No Alloc node is inserted, the MaterializationPass
        inserts Materialize+Initialize at the right position, and MemoryPlanning /
        FreeInsertion / InplaceOptimization can then plan, reuse, and free its
        buffer (unlike create_*_tensor, which is allocated eagerly and so is opaque
        to those passes). Use this for graph/loop-body scratch you want the memory
        passes to manage.

        :param name: The tensor's name.
        :param dims: Its extents.
        :param intermediate: Whether nothing reads the tensor after ``execute()``. A tensor declared without it is a RESULT: the caller may read it once the graph has run, so it has to exist whole then, and ``AxisTiling`` leaves it whole however large, naming it in its report when it is over the memory budget. Declare every tensor the program builds only for its own use as an intermediate, so the graph may stream it in blocks and never hold it whole.
        :param backing: Storage the tensor lives in, as ``annotate_backing`` takes it. Passing it here is the same as annotating the declared tensor, except that a refused backing leaves nothing declared.
        :returns: The declared tensor, owned by this graph.
        :raises Exception: As ``annotate_backing``, when ``backing`` is refused.


    .. py:method:: declare_zero_runtime_tensor(name: str, shape: list[SpaceDim], intermediate: bool = False, backing: TensorBacking | None = None) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor
                   declare_zero_runtime_tensor(name: str, dims: list[int], intermediate: bool = False, backing: TensorBacking | None = None) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Declare a deferred graph-owned tensor whose shape is stated in index spaces.

        The three statements a space-shaped scratch tensor needs today, collapsed into the one
        that cannot disagree with itself:

        .. code-block:: text

           // Today - three calls, three chances to drift apart:
           auto &T = graph.declare_zero_runtime_tensor<double>("T", {4, 8});
           graph.annotate_spaces(T, {occ, virt});
           graph.annotate_dims(T, {"occ", "virt"});

           // Here:
           auto &T = graph.declare_zero_runtime_tensor<double>("T", {occ, virt});

        **What the axes are annotated with**

        A space-typed axis is annotated with that space AND given a dim symbol, so the tensor is
        rebindable at new extents without a second call. The symbol is the space's
        ``IndexSpace::dim_symbol``, declared once at registration; a space registered without one
        is refused here rather than having a name invented for it. A numeric axis is left literal
        and unannotated, which is what a fixed size means.

        .. seealso::

           SpaceDim

        .. seealso::

           annotate_dims

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param name: Human-readable name.
        :param shape: One entry per axis: an index space, or a number for an axis that is genuinely a fixed size. ``SpaceDim`` converts from both.
        :param intermediate: Whether the tensor is graph-internal scratch.
        :param backing: A write backing; see the dims overload.
        :returns: Reference to the deferred tensor.
        :raises Exception: If a named space has no extent this graph has learned (see ``space_extent``): either nothing annotated over it yet, in which case ``pin_space_extent`` says so directly, or two annotated axes disagree, which is what a ragged family looks like and wants ``annotate_ragged_dim`` instead.


    .. py:method:: declare_zero_tiled_tensor(name: str, tile_sizes: list[list[int]], intermediate: bool = False) -> TiledRuntimeTensor
                   declare_zero_tiled_tensor(name: str, shape: list[SpaceTiling], intermediate: bool = False) -> TiledRuntimeTensor

        Tiled analog of declare_zero_runtime_tensor(): a graph-owned
        TiledRuntimeTensor shell over ``tile_sizes`` with DEFERRED lifecycle.

        The shell starts with no populated tiles - ops create tiles on demand
        (infer-and-create, zeroed) - so Materialize and Initialize are cheap,
        but registering the handle as Deferred puts the tensor under the same
        lifecycle machinery as dense scratch: Materialization hoists the pair
        to the loop's parent, and FreeInsertion can release the tile storage
        after the last consumer (release() keeps the sparsity pattern, so a
        later replay re-materializes into the same structure). MemoryPlanning
        leaves tiled scratch alone by construction: the arena requires
        materialize_into, and a tile-wise tensor has no single buffer to
        place. Scratch REUSE per replay is safe for the ops with the
        leftover-scale rule (einsum's c_pf, direct_division's and permute's
        beta apply to every stored tile, so stale tiles from a previous
        iteration are zeroed, not kept).


    .. py:method:: declare_internal_tensor(name: str, dims: list[int]) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Declare a deferred intermediate on a caller's behalf.

        For a helper that adds tensors the caller never sees, such as the partial products of an
        einsum over three or more operands. It is an ordinary declared intermediate to every pass,
        so ``AxisTiling`` may build it a block at a time and ``Materialization`` gives it storage
        or leaves it without any once nothing uses it. Unlike a tensor the caller declared, it does
        not make a graph run without those passes fail: its first ``execute()`` gives it storage
        when a node still reads or writes it and no Materialize node does.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param name: The tensor's name.
        :param dims: Its extents.
        :returns: The declared tensor, owned by this graph.


    .. py:method:: scratch_runtime(name: str, dims: list[int]) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Runtime-rank scratch (see scratch(); pybind-facing analog).


    .. py:method:: scratch_zero_runtime(name: str, dims: list[int]) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Runtime-rank zero-initialized scratch.


    .. py:method:: bind_begin() -> None

        Begin a multi-slot bind, to be finished by ``bind_commit``.

        ``bind`` is variadic and cannot be bound to Python, and calling the one-pair spelling
        once per slot is NOT the same operation: a dim symbol is a constraint ACROSS slots, so
        a caller moving a problem's extents has to hand over every slot before any of them is
        reconciled. Binding them one at a time solves each against a half-updated interface and
        fails with operands that "describe two different problems".

        These three calls are that transaction, opened up so a caller without variadics can
        drive it: ``bind_begin``, then ``bind_add`` once per slot, then ``bind_commit``.
        Nothing is repointed until the commit, so a refusal leaves the graph as it was.

        .. note::

           Each added tensor must outlive the commit; only a reference is kept.

        .. seealso::

           bind_add

        .. seealso::

           bind_commit

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: bind_add(name: str, tensor: Any) -> None

        Add one slot to the bind opened by ``bind_begin``.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param name: The manifest name of the slot.
        :param tensor: The storage to bind. Must outlive the commit.
        :raises Exception: When no bind is open.


    .. py:method:: bind_commit() -> None

        Finish the bind opened by ``bind_begin``, as one transaction.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :raises Exception: As ``bind`` does, with nothing repointed.


    .. py:method:: bind_slot(name: str, tensor: Any) -> None

        Bind ONE named slot, the spelling a binding can carry.

        ``bind`` is variadic, which is right for a C++ caller and unbindable from
        Python; this is the same operation with one pair, applied through the same
        solver. Call it once per slot.

        .. seealso::

           bind

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param name: The manifest name of the slot.
        :param tensor: The storage to bind.


    .. py:method:: manifest_names() -> list[str]

        Every manifest binding key, in the manifest's own order.

        The whole ``InterfaceManifest`` is a richer object than a binding needs;
        this is the part a caller binds by, and it is what the Python surface
        exposes so a driver can walk a loaded graph's interface without the
        manifest type having to be bound too.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The names.


    .. py:method:: manifest() -> list[ManifestEntry]

        Every slot of ``manifest`` once, in its order: what Python sees as ``manifest()``.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :returns: The entries, an ``InOut`` slot once.


    .. py:method:: bind_ragged_extents(name: str, axis: int, extents: list[int]) -> None

        Supply the per-instance extents of one ragged axis.

        A ragged axis (see ``annotate_ragged_dim``) has no scalar extent to solve, so this is
        what a caller hands the graph in place of one: the design's "``bind`` supplies a
        per-instance extent table rather than a scalar".

        .. note::

           ACCEPTED and STORED, not yet consumed, and the task is larger than it first
           looked. The nodes that read such a table are the grouped/batched ones, and the
           original plan was that the resource phase re-forms them at load from the table.
           That assumes grouped nodes are a resource-phase PRODUCT. They are not:
           ``grouped_batched_gemm`` is a capture-time API and the workload that needs this
           (DLPNO) calls it directly, so there is no pre-resource algebraic form underneath
           to fall back to. Worse, a grouped batch partitions its members by SHAPE at
           capture and reorders the operand lists to match, so moving the extents changes
           the partition itself and not merely the numbers in it.

        Consuming the table therefore waits on a decision recorded in the design doc:
        either capture the per-member algebraic form and add a pass that groups it, or
        keep the grouped form and teach it to re-derive its own partition. Until then a
        graph containing a grouped node refuses to save, saying so, and the table is
        available through ``ragged_extent_tables`` and nothing else reads it.

        .. seealso::

           clear_bindings

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param name: The manifest name of the slot.
        :param axis: Which axis of that slot the table describes.
        :param extents: One extent per instance of the axis's space, in instance order.
        :raises Exception: If ``name`` is not a manifest entry; if ``axis`` is past its rank or is not declared ragged; if ``extents`` is empty; or if another table already stored for the SAME space has a different length. The length is the instance count, which is a property of the space (how many pairs there are) and not of one operand, so two tables over one space disagreeing about it describes nothing.


    .. py:attribute:: space_registry
        :type: SpaceRegistry

        The registry this graph resolves index spaces against.

        A graph annotated against one registry must be queried against the same one: a
        ``SpaceId`` is a handle into the registry that issued it and means nothing anywhere else.
        The process-global registry is the default precisely so that the common case never has to
        think about it; a test (or a capture that wants an isolated set of spaces) overrides it.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: explain
        :type: str

        What the last optimize() did, as a human-readable report.

        Node counts before/after plus the per-pass highlights (chains
        restructured with estimated speedups, buffers merged in place, Frees
        inserted, arena size vs the buffers it hosts, batches formed and
        profitability-gate skips). Empty until optimize() has run.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: decisions
        :type: list[Decision]

        What the last optimize() decided, as records: the ones ``explain`` prints from.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: name
        :type: str

        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: accuracy_budget_value
        :type: float

        The budget's value, for a caller that cannot hold an optional pair.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: planned_thread_count
        :type: int

        Threads the per-node widths on this graph were planned against.

        A width is chosen for one machine at one moment: the planner divides a
        known number of threads between the nodes, so the same widths on a
        different thread count are not a worse plan, they are a wrong one.
        Recording the count the plan assumed lets ``DataflowExecutor`` notice
        and fall back to running every node at width 1.

        0 means nothing recorded, which is what a hand-set width or an unplanned
        graph looks like; those are honored as they are found. Deliberately not
        serialized with the graph, for the same reason the widths are not.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: thread_replan_armed
        :type: bool

        Whether the thread plan is still inside its measured trial window: a
        re-plan is waiting for the next completed replay, or the trial replays
        are still being timed. False once the widths are final. @see plan_threads


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: has_setup
        :type: bool

        Whether this graph carries at least one setup node.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: setup_key
        :type: str

        The key ``set_setup_key`` last recorded.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: GrowthClass

    How a space's extent grows with the size of the system.

    The extent scales as ``N^exponent`` for system size ``N`` (``constant``, ``linear``, ``power``).
    Advisory: lets a pass rank contraction orders before extents are bound.


    .. py:staticmethod:: constant() -> GrowthClass

        A space whose extent does not grow with the system (exponent 0).

        :returns: The constant growth class.


    .. py:staticmethod:: linear() -> GrowthClass

        A space whose extent grows proportionally with the system (exponent 1).

        :returns: The linear growth class.


    .. py:staticmethod:: power(exponent: float) -> GrowthClass

        A space whose extent grows as a user-chosen power of the system size.

        :param exponent: The exponent to use.
        :returns: The requested growth class.


    .. py:attribute:: exponent
        :type: float

        Exponent of the system size that the extent follows. Defaults to linear growth.

        Read-only from Python, as a space's semantics are fixed at registration.


.. py:class:: IndexSpace

    The semantics of one index space, and nothing else.

    Field-wise equality makes a repeated identical registration idempotent and a conflicting one an
    error. An aggregate for designated initializers; Python uses ``make_index_space``.


    .. py:attribute:: name
        :type: str

        Human-readable name, unique within a registry ("occ", "virt", "aux", "grid", ...).

    .. py:attribute:: scale_symbol
        :type: str

        Letter this space contributes to a symbolic cost polynomial ("o", "v", "x", "g", ...).

    .. py:attribute:: dim_symbol
        :type: str

        Name a SYMBOLIC EXTENT over this space goes by ("no", "nv", ...). Empty when the space has
        none, and then an axis over it cannot be given a symbolic extent from the space alone.

        Distinct from ``scale_symbol`` (the cost-polynomial letter) and ``name``. A ragged space
        uses ``"ragged:<space>"`` instead.

    .. py:attribute:: typical_extent
        :type: float

        Advisory extent, used only to break ties when no tensor instance is bound. Zero means unset.

    .. py:attribute:: growth
        :type: GrowthClass

        How the extent grows with system size.


.. py:class:: InplaceOptimization

    InplaceOptimization pass: in-place storage merging for elementwise consumers.

    When an element-aligned elementwise node (DirectProduct, DirectDivision, Axpby with beta == 0, or a
    member of their grouped forms) pure-overwrites a graph-owned intermediate while reading another
    graph-owned intermediate for the LAST time, the output reuses the dying input's storage: the graph
    metadata merges the two ids (CSE-style rewrite), the output's lifecycle nodes are dropped, and its
    executor slot is durably redirected. One buffer allocation and its write-traffic disappear per merge -
    the CC amplitude-update pattern (R -> Tnew = R * invD) is the canonical win.

    This pass is in the default pipeline, run before FreeInsertion / MemoryPlanning so each merge removes
    a buffer and shortens the intervals those liveness passes then work with.

    **Soundness guards**

    - Consumer whitelist: only ops whose out[i] depends solely on element i of the inputs may alias output with input (contractions and permutes never qualify). Pure overwrite is detected via the out-tensor-as-input recording convention. A grouped member may only reuse one of its own inputs, and no member of the node may read the destination, because the members run at once.
    - Both tensors: graph-owned intermediates owning their storage (not views, not redirected onto another tensor, not viewed by anyone), identical dims, dtype and byte size. The source has exactly one producer, which runs before the consumer, and dies at the consumer; the destination has exactly one writer, no Initialize, and no reader ahead of the consumer.
    - Reads and writes are counted per buffer (Graph::buffer_of), so a node reading a tensor through a redirected slot counts as a reader of the storage the slot was redirected to.
    - Graphs containing control flow at this level are skipped (bodies reference parent tensors invisibly to plain use-counts); bodies are processed on their own recursion level. GPU-placed graphs are skipped (device shadows swap buffers behind the slots).

    num_candidates() still reports the single-producer/single-consumer census this pass exposed when it
    was analysis-only.

    **Example (C++)**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("inplace");
       auto     &R    = graph.scratch<double, 2>("R", n, n);       // residual (dies at the update)
       auto     &invD = graph.scratch<double, 2>("invD", n, n);    // 1/denominator
       auto     &Tnew = graph.scratch<double, 2>("Tnew", n, n);    // amplitude update
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           cg::einsum("ij <- ik ; kj", 0.0, &R, 1.0, A, B);        // produce R
           cg::direct_product(1.0, R, invD, 0.0, &Tnew);           // Tnew = R (*) invD, R read last
       }
       graph.apply(cg::PassManager::create_default());             // InplaceOptimization runs here
       // Tnew now reuses R's storage; R's buffer and its write-traffic are gone.

    **Example (Python)**

    Constructible standalone (getters are properties). A whitelisted elementwise consumer merges; an
    einsum consumer never does (nothing merges, num_merged stays 0).

    .. code-block:: py

       import einsums, einsums.graph as cg
       g = cg.Graph("inplace")
       R    = g.create_zero_tensor("R",    [n, n], dtype="float64")
       invD = g.create_random_tensor("invD", [n, n], dtype="float64")
       Tnew = g.create_zero_tensor("Tnew", [n, n], dtype="float64")
       with cg.capture(g):
           einsums.einsum("ij <- ik ; kj", R, A, B)
           einsums.linalg.direct_product(1.0, R, invD, 0.0, Tnew)
       p  = cg.InplaceOptimization()
       pm = cg.PassManager(); pm.add(p); pm.run(g)                 # or g.apply(cg.default_pass_manager())
       # p.num_merged, p.num_candidates  (properties, not methods)

    **Limitations**

    - Only the element-aligned elementwise consumers DirectProduct, DirectDivision and Axpby(beta == 0), and the grouped forms of the three, may alias output with a dying input; contractions (Einsum/Gemm/BatchedGemm) and permutes, grouped or not, are excluded and never merge.
    - Both tensors must be graph-owned intermediates, non-viewed, with identical dims and byte size; the source needs exactly one producer and this node as its only reader, the destination exactly one writer and a pure overwrite (destination not read).
    - HOST-only and single-level: any graph with control flow at this level, or with GPU placement / host<->device transfers, is skipped (bodies are handled on their own recursion level).
    - A destination carrying an Initialize node is skipped rather than merged; the v1 pass does not delete the (now-dead) Initialize.

    **Future improvements**

    - Handle a destination with an Initialize by deleting the dead init instead of bailing on the merge.
    - Extend beyond the elementwise whitelist to any consumer whose access is provably element-aligned.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_candidates
        :type: int

        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_merged
        :type: int

        Number of output buffers merged into dying inputs in the last run.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: LaplaceTransform

    Substitute a quadrature for a tagged energy denominator and decouple its consumer.

    .. seealso::

       LaplaceTransform.hpp for the mathematics, the tag and every refusal

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

        Default-construct. Explicit so the binding codegen has a constructor to annotate.


    .. py:staticmethod:: tag_name() -> str

        The provenance tag this pass claims.

        :returns: ``"laplace_denominator"``.


    .. py:method:: add_energy(name: str, vector: Any) -> None

        Hand the pass one of the orbital-energy vectors the tags name.

        Supplied to the PASS rather than found in the graph, which is the shape
        ``MetricFitFactorization`` already has and for the same reason: the energies go into
        the reciprocal before the capture starts, so no node mentions them and the graph has
        never seen them. Registering them on the graph instead would leave it holding a handle
        nothing uses beside the one the setup body captures, two tensors under one name, and a
        manifest binds by name.

        The vector becomes an interface tensor of the transformed graph, under this name,
        because the setup body captures it. That is what lets a saved graph refit its quadrature
        at a new geometry: a bind moves the energies and the next execute rebuilds the rule.

        :param name: The name the denominator's tag uses for this axis.
        :param vector: The energies. Its storage must outlive every replay, since the setup node reads it on each bind.
        :raises Exception: When ``vector`` is not a non-empty rank-1 tensor, or when a different vector is already registered under ``name``.


    .. py:method:: add_energy_f32(name: str, vector: Any) -> None

        The same, for a single-precision problem.

        :param name: The name the denominator's tag uses for this axis.
        :param vector: The energies.


    .. py:method:: clear_energies() -> None

        Forget every energy vector handed over so far.


    .. py:staticmethod:: denominator_tag(energies: list[str], signs: str) -> ProvenanceTag

        Build the tag a caller puts on a reciprocal denominator.

        A helper rather than a documented convention, so the pass reads the attributes out of
        the same place a caller writes them in.

        :param energies: One orbital-energy tensor NAME per axis of the denominator, in axis order. Names rather than ids, because they are what a manifest binds by and what lets a saved graph refit at a new geometry.
        :param signs: One character per axis, ``'+'`` or ``'-'``, saying whether that axis's energy enters the denominator added or subtracted.
        :returns: The tag, ready for ``Graph::annotate_tag``.
        :raises Exception: When the two lists differ in length, when either is empty, or when ``signs`` holds a character that is neither ``'+'`` nor ``'-'``.


    .. py:staticmethod:: error_tensor_name(denominator: str) -> str

        The tensor a bind's measured quadrature error is written to.

        A function rather than a convention, for the reason
        ``MetricFitFactorization::dropped_param_name`` is one: a caller reads the number from
        the same place the graph writes it. The approximation record carries the error measured
        at OPTIMIZE time, over the range the energies then had; this is what the range currently
        bound actually achieves, and the two differ exactly when a rebind widened the interval.

        :param denominator: The tagged tensor's name.
        :returns: The name of a one-element tensor the setup body writes on every bind.


    .. py:method:: set_epsilon(epsilon: float) -> None

        The target relative accuracy of the quadrature.

        Overrides ``einsums:graph:laplace-epsilon`` for this pass object, which is the per-
        pipeline half of the knob convention every rewrite knob here follows.

        :param epsilon: The tolerance. Must be finite and strictly between zero and one.
        :raises Exception: When it is not.


    .. py:method:: set_points(points: int) -> None
