       # Reorder exposes no result counter; it only permutes the node order to lower peak memory.

    **Limitations**

    - It **reschedules only**: it never fuses, splits, or eliminates nodes, and it cannot lower peak memory below what the fixed node set and its true dependencies allow.
    - The net-score ranking is a **greedy local heuristic**, not a globally optimal peak-memory schedule. The peak check only guarantees it is never worse than the order given.
    - The peak it compares is a model: it ignores FreeInsertion's size floor, so a tensor below it counts as freed here but stays allocated at run time.
    - If the dependency graph is cyclic or otherwise leaves nodes unscheduled (`order.size() != n`), the pass bails and leaves the node order untouched.
    - Tensor sizes come from `TensorHandle::total_bytes()`; the pass runs before Materialization, so sizes are taken from declared dims rather than realized allocations.

    **Future improvements**

    - Replace the greedy last-consumer heuristic with a cost-model-driven schedule that minimizes true peak footprint (e.g. lookahead or a liveness-interval formulation) instead of a per-step local choice.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None


.. py:class:: RunMetrics

    What the last replay of a graph did, as ``Graph::last_run`` reports it.

    Measured, beside what the passes planned: ``peak_bytes`` is what
    ``passes::MemoryPlanning::peak_memory`` predicts, and a backing's bytes are what
    SlabStreaming's report estimates.

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:attribute:: wall_ms
        :type: float

        Wall-clock milliseconds of the whole replay.

    .. py:attribute:: nodes
        :type: list[NodeRun]

        Every node the replay ran, the graph's own and its bodies'. Each graph's nodes are in the
        order they first ran, and a body's come before those of the graph holding it, since a body's
        replay finishes first.

    .. py:attribute:: backings
        :type: list[BackingRun]

        One entry per backing in the graph and its bodies, in name order.

    .. py:attribute:: peak_bytes
        :type: int

        The most bytes of graph-managed tensors held at once: deferred tensors the graph allocated
        and freed, plus ``arena_bytes``. Not the process's memory: BLAS scratch is outside it, and
        the caller's own tensors are in ``resident_bytes``.

    .. py:attribute:: arena_bytes
        :type: int

        The arena MemoryPlanning placed intermediates in, held for the whole replay; 0 without one.

    .. py:attribute:: resident_bytes
        :type: int

        What the replay held from its start to its end outside ``peak_bytes``: the caller's
        tensors and any intermediate given storage outside a lifecycle node (@ref
        Graph::resident_bytes). The program held ``peak_bytes`` plus this at its peak.


.. py:class:: ScaleAbsorption

    Scale-elimination pass: removes a Scale by deleting it (dead) or folding its factor into a consumer's prefactor (live).

    A `scale` is in-place (`C *= α`), so its live range ends at the tensor's next
    writer. It is eliminated in two ways:

    **Dead-scale removal** — the next node writing the tensor overwrites it
    without reading its prior contents (c_prefactor / beta == 0) and nothing reads
    it in between, so the scale's result is discarded wholesale:

    - **Einsum**: Scale(α) + Einsum(c_pf=0) → Einsum(c_pf=0)
    - **BatchedGemm**: Scale(α) + BatchedGemm(beta=0) → BatchedGemm(beta=0)
    - **Permute**: Scale(α) + Permute(beta=0) → Permute(beta=0)

    **Fold (live scale)** — the scaled tensor is overwritten later, so its scaled
    value is observable only to the nodes in between. Every one of those nodes
    takes the factor and the Scale is dropped. Two ways a node can take it:

    - **Operand**: it reads the tensor as one operand. einsum and axpby are linear in their source, so `α` folds into `ab_prefactor` / `alpha`: Scale(α, A) + Einsum(ab_pf, …, A, B) + overwrite(A) → Einsum(ab_pf·α, …, A, B)
    - **Accumulator**: it accumulates into the tensor and reads it only as that destination. `scale(α, C)` then `C = c_pf·C + …` is exactly `C = (c_pf·α)·C + …`, so `α` folds into `c_prefactor` / `beta`: Scale(α, C) + Einsum(c_pf≠0, C, …) → Einsum(c_pf·α, C, …)

    The fold is all-or-nothing across the window: if any observer cannot take the
    factor the whole Scale is kept, because a partial fold would be wrong rather
    than merely a missed optimization.

    The fold is written to BOTH the live EinsumParams (read by the CPU executor)
    and the descriptor snapshot (read by GPU dispatch / later passes); a
    descriptor-only edit would desync them — the reason only ops with live shared
    params are folded into. Because the fold removes a writer that the einsum
    read, it deliberately flips that read to the tensor's initial contents; the
    pass declares the `(einsum, tensor)` pair via ``compensated_reads`` so the
    program-order validator waives its structural guard for exactly that read.

    **Example (C++)**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("scale_absorption");
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           cg::scale(3.0, &A);                                 // A *= 3
           cg::einsum("ij <- ik ; kj", 0.0, &C, 1.0, A, B);    // C = 3·(A·B)  -- A is the sole reader
           cg::einsum("ik <- i ; k", 0.0, &A, 1.0, x, y);      // A overwritten (its scaled value is dead)
       }
       graph.apply(cg::PassManager::create_default());         // ScaleAbsorption runs here
       // The Scale is gone and the first einsum's ab_prefactor is now 3.

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       import einsums, einsums.graph as cg
       g = cg.Graph("scale_absorption")
       with cg.capture(g):
           einsums.linalg.scale(3.0, A)                        # A *= 3
           einsums.einsum("ij <- ik ; kj", C, A, B)            # C = 3·(A·B)
           einsums.einsum("ik <- i ; k", A, x, y)              # A overwritten
       sa = cg.ScaleAbsorption()
       pm = cg.PassManager(); pm.add(sa)
       g.apply(pm)                                             # or cg.default_pass_manager()
       # sa.num_absorbed  -> 1   (getters are exposed as properties, not methods)

    **Limitations**

    - Only **einsum** and **axpby** can take the factor. `gemm`, `permute` and `BatchedGemm` bake their prefactors into the executor closure, so folding into them is not desync-safe (they lack live shared params); a scale whose window contains one of them is kept.
    - The tensor must be read as exactly ONE operand by each observer: the tensor used as both einsum operands, or accumulated into AND used as an operand by the same node, would need α² and is left un-folded.
    - A scaled value that is never overwritten stays observable to the caller after execute, so its Scale is always kept.
    - A scale whose buffer is also accessed through a VIEW while the scaled value is live is kept. A slice consumer's prefactor scales only its own span, where the scale covers the whole buffer, so the scale's effect on the regions no slice touches (padding, unused slots) is observable and nothing can stand in for it. This holds for the dead-scale route too: a later whole-tensor overwrite does not make the scale dead when a view write in between read the scaled bytes.
    - Only ops with live shared params can take the factor at all, so the grouped ops (GroupedBatchedGemm, GroupedDot, GroupedAxpby) and every kind this pass does not enumerate veto rather than fold.
    - Reads from inside a control-flow node's sub-graphs count (via ``Graph::effective_io``) but cannot be folded into, so a loop body reading the scaled tensor keeps the Scale.

    **Future improvements**

    - Fold into `permute` / `BatchedGemm` / `gemm` once they carry live prefactor params the way EinsumParams and AxpbyParams do (CG audit §5.1-3).


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_absorbed
        :type: int

        Number of scale nodes eliminated in the last run (dead-removed or folded).


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: ScalingAnalysis

    Report how a program scales: every contraction's cost polynomial, and what limits it.

    The user-facing face of the symbolic cost model. It walks the graph, asks
    ``symbolic_cost_for`` for each contraction's cost, adds up the flops, sizes the intermediates,
    and names the node whose polynomial dominates. Nothing is rewritten and nothing is decided; the
    point is to answer "what is the rate-limiting term" beside the question "how fast is this", and
    to be the first consumer that proves a program's space annotations are the ones its author
    meant.

    Three surfaces carry the same numbers:

    - ``explain``, the two or three lines that join ``graph.explain()``,
    - ``print_report``, the full per-node table,
    - the accessors (``node_costs``, ``total_flops``, ``rate_limiting``, ...) for tests and tooling.

    **What is analysed**

    Contraction nodes only. Every other kind is counted in ``skip_reasons`` and contributes
    nothing: inventing a cost formula for an op whose cost model has not been written would put
    numbers in the report that no pass agrees with.

    **Where the letters come from**

    A node's cost is derived from the spaces its operands' handles carry AT THE TIME THE PASS RUNS,
    not from the ``letter_spaces`` map frozen into the node at capture. That is what makes the
    report reflect a declaration that arrived after capture, and everything ``SpacePropagation``
    inferred immediately before this pass in the default pipeline. Letters that no annotated slot
    reaches, and letters whose slots contradict each other, fall back to the node's captured map and
    then to an anonymous per-letter variable, so an unannotated program still yields a complete
    report, just one written in ``?i`` rather than in ``o``.

    **Example (C++)**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("scaling");
       graph.set_space_registry(registry);
       graph.annotate_spaces(T2, {occ, occ, virt, virt});
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           cg::einsum("ijab <- ijcd ; cdab", &R2, T2, Vvvvv);
       }
       auto [modified, scaling] = graph.apply<cg::passes::ScalingAnalysis>();
       // scaling.total_flops().to_string(&registry) == "2*o^2*v^4"
       scaling.print_report(std::cout);

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       scaling = cg.ScalingAnalysis()
       pm = cg.PassManager()
       pm.add(scaling)
       pm.run(g)
       assert scaling.total_flops_str() == "2*o^2*v^4"
       print(scaling.report_string())

    Python sees the polynomials as their renderings rather than as ``SymbolicPoly`` objects; see
    the rendered surface on this class.

    **Limitations**

    - Analysis only. ``run`` always reports "not modified".
    - ``memory_bound`` is the SUM of the intermediate sizes, which is an upper bound on the high-water mark and not the high-water mark itself. A liveness-aware figure needs the interval analysis ``MemoryPlanning`` already does for bytes, applied to polynomials, and that is a later task. Read it as "no more than this", never as "this much".
    - A loop body is analysed once, not once per iteration: a trip count is a runtime quantity and multiplying a polynomial by it would put a number in the report that the symbolic layer cannot defend. Node and tensor entries carry their graph's name so a body's contribution stays attributable.
    - An intermediate is sized from the index letters of the contraction that writes it. An intermediate no contraction writes gets no entry, and is counted in ``skip_reasons``.
    - The rate-limiting verdict ranks flops only, through the same total order the structural passes use. Two nodes tie only when their flop polynomials are literally identical, so a tie is a statement about the program rather than an artifact of the comparison.

    **Future improvements**

    - A liveness-aware memory high-water polynomial, once intervals and polynomials meet.
    - Cost formulas for the non-contraction kinds that have one, starting with Gemm and the element-wise family.
    - A Python surface for the polynomials themselves, once ``SymbolicPoly`` is worth binding as a type. Today Python reads their renderings.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:method:: report_string() -> str

        The same report ``print_report`` writes, returned as a string.

        The form a caller that is not holding a stream wants, which is every Python caller and every
        test that asserts on what the report says. Streams are not bound, and a returned string
        needs no flushing to be readable.

        :returns: The report. Never empty; it says so when nothing was analysed.


    .. py:method:: total_flops_str() -> str

        The rendering of ``total_flops``.

        :returns: The polynomial as text, in the analysed graph's scale symbols.


    .. py:method:: total_traffic_str() -> str

        The rendering of ``total_traffic``.

        :returns: The polynomial as text, in the analysed graph's scale symbols.


    .. py:method:: memory_bound_str() -> str

        The rendering of ``memory_bound``.

        :returns: The polynomial as text. See the class documentation for what the bound does not say.


    .. py:method:: node_labels() -> list[str]

        The label of every analysed node, in the order they were visited.

        :returns: The labels, parallel to ``node_flops``.


    .. py:method:: node_flops() -> list[str]

        The rendered flops polynomial of every analysed node, in the order they were visited.

        :returns: The renderings, parallel to ``node_labels``.


    .. py:method:: rate_limiting_labels() -> list[str]

        The labels of the rate-limiting nodes.

        :returns: The labels. More than one entry only when their flop polynomials are identical.


    .. py:method:: intermediate_names() -> list[str]

        The name of every sized intermediate, in the order they were visited.

        :returns: The names, parallel to ``intermediate_sizes_str``.


    .. py:method:: intermediate_sizes_str() -> list[str]

        The rendered element-count polynomial of every sized intermediate.

        :returns: The renderings, parallel to ``intermediate_names``.


    .. py:attribute:: num_unannotated_nodes
        :type: int

        How many analysed nodes carry at least one unannotated letter.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_analyzed
        :type: int

        How many nodes were analysed.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: ScratchPrivatization

    Break false dependencies from reused scratch buffers by renaming each reuse onto its own clone.

    Captured code that recycles one scratch tensor through many unrelated write→read episodes -- the CCSD idiom
    ``for each term: tmp = contract(...); r2 += tmp`` -- serializes the whole graph: every overwrite of ``tmp``
    carries a WAR edge from the previous episode's readers and a WAW edge from its writer, so operations that
    share no data still execute strictly one after another and a parallel executor finds no width.

    The pass splits each such tensor's accesses into *generations* (a generation starts at every pure overwrite:
    a node that writes the whole tensor without reading it) and renames the interior generations onto a bounded
    pool of clones, round-robin. The LAST generation keeps the original tensor, so every value observable after
    the graph (or one loop-body replay) completes is unchanged -- which is what makes the rewrite safe without
    knowing who else holds the tensor: outside observers can only look between executions, and between
    executions the original buffer holds exactly the value it always did.

    Clones are declared as graph-owned deferred intermediates (Materialization allocates them, FreeInsertion
    and MemoryPlanning manage them like any other scratch), and the pool is capped -- more clones than the
    machine has cores adds memory without adding parallelism.

    A tensor is left alone whenever renaming can't be proven safe locally:

    - any access through a view (a partial write is not a generation boundary),
    - any read before the first pure overwrite (the value is carried in from outside, e.g. across iterations),
    - any touch by a control-flow or lifecycle node, or by a node kind the pass cannot rebuild,
    - fewer than two generations (nothing to split).

    Renaming a node means rebuilding its executor: captured executors resolve operands through TensorSlot
    pointers baked at capture time, so editing ``Node::inputs`` alone would change the schedule but not the
    computation. Einsum nodes are rebuilt through ``Graph::make_einsum_node``;
    Permute and Axpby nodes get executors that resolve their operands by TensorId at call time.

    In the default pipeline (after LoopInvariantHoisting, before ContractionPlanning): late enough that the
    structural rewrites (SymmetrizedAccumulation, LCCF, hoisting) have settled, early enough that the planning
    and memory passes see the renamed graph. Recursion into loop bodies is innermost-first and self-driven.

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       import einsums, einsums.graph as cg
       g = cg.Graph("sp")
       body = g.add_loop("iter", n, cont)
       with cg.capture(body):
           einsums.einsum("ij <- ik ; kj", tmp, A1, B1); einsums.linalg.axpby(1.0, tmp, 1.0, acc)
           einsums.einsum("ij <- ik ; kj", tmp, A2, B2); einsums.linalg.axpby(1.0, tmp, 1.0, acc)
       body.set_executor(cg.DataflowExecutor())   # BEFORE apply: the pass rewrites only executor-carrying graphs
       g.apply(cg.default_pass_manager())         # the two contractions can now overlap

    **Limitations**

    - Only Einsum, Permute (single-input) and Axpby nodes are rebuilt; a generation touched by any other node kind disqualifies the tensor. Notably a SymmetrizedAccumulation-rewritten permute (two inputs) is skipped deliberately: its descriptor no longer matches its baked executor.
    - Trusts declared node I/O, like every other pass; an executor touching a tensor it does not declare was already unsound under Reorder.
    - No profitability model beyond the pool cap: with the sequential executor the rewrite only spends memory.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None
                   __init__(max_copies: int) -> None


        :param max_copies: Clone-pool cap per tensor; 0 means "number of hardware threads".


    .. py:method:: set_require_executor(require: bool) -> None

        By default the pass rewrites only graphs that carry an installed
        executor (Graph::set_executor): under the built-in sequential replay
        the clones cost cache locality and buy nothing, so a graph that will
        never run wide is left alone.

        Install the executor BEFORE apply().
        Setting this false privatizes unconditionally (tests, custom drivers).


    .. py:attribute:: num_tensors_privatized
        :type: int

        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_copies_created
        :type: int

        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_nodes_rebuilt
        :type: int

        (read-only)


.. py:class:: SequentialExecutor

    Sequential executor, executes nodes in topological order.

    This is the default execution strategy. Nodes run one at a time
    in dependency order. Zero overhead, no thread safety concerns.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None


.. py:class:: SerializabilityBlocker

    One node that blocks a graph from being saved, and what blocks it.

    .. seealso::

       Graph::serializability_report


    .. py:attribute:: node_id
        :type: int

        The offending node's id.

    .. py:attribute:: label
        :type: str

        Its human-readable label, so the report names something a user recognises.

    .. py:attribute:: kind_name
        :type: str

        ``op_kind_name`` of its kind.

        :type: str

        Empty for a node of that graph itself. Otherwise a ``/``-separated path of the
        control-flow nodes descended through, each named by its label and, for a
        ``Conditional``, by which branch was taken: ``loop(scf_iter)/then(converged)``.

        A NodeId is graph-local, so ``node_id`` alone does not locate a node inside a body.
        This is what makes the pair addressable.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


.. py:class:: SlabStreaming

    Read a backed tensor a slab at a time instead of loading it whole.

    A tensor annotated with a backing (``Graph::annotate_backing``) is loaded whole before its
    first reader, which needs memory for all of it. This pass rewrites each contraction that reads
    one into a loop over blocks of the backed tensor's LAST axis: every iteration reads one slab
    from the storage into a buffer the size of the block, then runs the same contraction on it.
    The last axis because the storage keeps elements column-major, so a block of the last axis is
    one contiguous extent, read in one piece. A backed tensor no node reads whole any more is then
    never allocated.

    What a block does to the destination follows the letter the last axis carries. When the
    destination carries it too, each block writes its own slice and the result is the captured
    one exactly. When the letter is summed, the blocks add into the destination: the first block
    applies the destination's prefactor and the rest add, which is the captured contraction's own
    order of operations with its sum cut into parts, so the result moves within the
    re-association tolerance.

    A tensor with a WRITE backing is streamed the other way. The contraction that produces it
    becomes a loop over blocks of its last axis: every iteration computes one block into a buffer
    the size of the block and writes that slab to the storage, so the tensor is never held whole.
    The contraction must overwrite the tensor (a destination prefactor of zero), be its only
    writer in memory, and read no backed tensor; otherwise the tensor is produced in memory and
    Materialization stores it whole after its last writer. Its readers are streamed as any backed
    tensor's are, but only once nothing writes it in memory, and they run after the writes: every
    read and write of a storage carries the storage's key.

    It acts only under a memory budget, the same one AxisTiling plans against: a backed tensor is
    streamed when it is larger than its share of ``--einsums:max-memory``
    (``--einsums:graph:tiling-budget-share``). One that fits is loaded once per replay, which beats
    one read per reading contraction. It runs after AxisTiling and inside every loop body, so a
    contraction AxisTiling placed in a block loop is streamed there.

    Declined, with the reason in the skip tally, and left to the whole load: a backed tensor whose
    last-axis letter is repeated in any operand or the destination; one read through a view other
    than a full one; a contraction that reads one backed tensor on both sides; one with permutation
    operators; any node but a contraction; operands that are not rank-erased tensors. A backed
    operand held in memory anyway, one that fits or that the graph wrote, is an ordinary operand,
    and of two backed operands both over their share the larger is streamed and the smaller held.

    The slab reads run in order with the contraction: a block's read finishes before its
    contraction starts. A graph this pass has rewritten cannot be saved, like one AxisTiling has
    rewritten; save the graph before the resource passes.

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

        Default-construct, reading the budget from the options.


    .. py:method:: set_slab_budget(bytes: int) -> None

        Override the slab budget for this pipeline, in bytes.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param bytes: A backed tensor larger than this is streamed, in slabs no larger than it. Zero turns the pass off; a negative value derives it from ``--einsums:max-memory`` and ``--einsums:graph:tiling-budget-share``, which is the default.


    .. py:method:: stored_order(name: str) -> list[int]

        The order the last apply left a backed tensor stored in.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

        :param name: The backed tensor's name.
        :returns: The declared axis at each stored position, as ``TensorBacking::stored_order`` gives it; empty for a tensor the apply moved no box of, or whose storage has no order.


    .. py:attribute:: name
        :type: str

        See ``OptimizerPass::name``.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_streamed
        :type: int

        How many contractions the last apply streamed.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: streamed
        :type: list[str]

        The backed tensors the last apply streamed, by name, one entry per streamed contraction.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_written
        :type: int

        How many of the streamed contractions produce their tensor into its storage.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: blocks
        :type: list[int]

        The block each streamed contraction reads, along the backed tensor's last axis.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_boxes
        :type: int

        How many views of a backed tensor the last apply turned into a read of the box each one names.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: SpaceDim

    One dimension of a declared tensor, given as an index space rather than a number.

    Converts implicitly from ``SpaceId`` so a shape reads as what its axes MEAN
    (``{occ, virt}``). An axis that is genuinely a fixed size is spelled ``fixed``
    (``{occ, fixed(3)}``).

    There is deliberately NO implicit conversion from an integer. A braced list of plain
    numbers must keep selecting the dims-based overload unambiguously, and an all-literal
    shape means the caller opted out of spaces entirely - ``fixed`` is how they say that
    about one axis while still naming spaces for the others.

    .. seealso::

       fixed

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__(s: SpaceId) -> None

        An axis over ``s``, sized from what the graph has learned about it.

        :param s: The index space.


    .. py:attribute:: space
        :type: SpaceId

        The space this axis ranges over, or an invalid id when the axis is a literal.

    .. py:attribute:: extent
        :type: int

        The extent, when ``space`` is invalid. Meaningless otherwise.


.. py:class:: SpaceId

    Opaque handle to a space held by a ``SpaceRegistry``.

    Stable for the life of the registry that issued it, meaningless anywhere else. A
    default-constructed id is invalid and every registry query rejects it.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

        Construct an invalid id.


    .. py:method:: __eq__(other: SpaceId) -> bool

        Whether two ids name the same space of the same registry.

        ``operator==`` as a named method, bound as Python's ``__eq__``.

        :param other: The id to compare against.
        :returns: True when both wrap the same registry-local index.


    .. py:attribute:: valid
        :type: bool

        Whether this id refers to a registered space.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: value
        :type: int

        The underlying registry-local index.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: SpacePropagation

    Infer per-slot index spaces on graph intermediates.

    Walks the graph in topological order and, for each node that produces a tensor, works out what
    each output slot ranges over from the annotations its operands carry. When every slot resolves,
    the output's ``TensorHandle::spaces`` is filled in and marked
    ``TensorHandle::spaces_inferred``. That is what keeps annotation effort proportional to a
    program's INPUTS rather than to its node count: a method annotates its handful of persistent
    tensors once, and every intermediate derived from them gets its spaces for free.

    **Current rules**

    - **Einsum**: the output slot takes the space of the letter that produced it, from the letters bound by the two input operands' slots.
    - **Scale**: ``C = α·A`` inherits A's spaces (a scale changes values, never what an axis ranges over).
    - **Permute / Transpose**: the output slots take the input's spaces reordered by the node's own index letters, so ``ji <- ij`` on an ``(occ, virt)`` tensor yields ``(virt, occ)``.
    - **Axpby**: the output takes the spaces every annotated input agrees on, slot by slot.

    **Scope**

    Only annotates graph-owned intermediates (``is_intermediate == true``). A user-owned tensor is
    never written to, and an unannotated INPUT never has a space pushed back onto it: inheritance
    across operands is deliberately off, because it is precisely how one wrong annotation spreads
    silently through the region a validation pass exists to police. Propagation flows from producer
    to output and nowhere else.

    An annotation the user DECLARED is never overwritten. One this pass or capture inferred may be
    refined, and re-inferring the same value is a no-op, so repeated runs converge.

    In the default pipeline it sits beside SymmetryPropagation, after Materialization and before GPU
    placement. The two are independent analyses of the same shape, and neither reads the other's
    output.

    **Example (C++)**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("space_propagation");
       auto     &T = graph.create_zero_tensor<double, 2>("T", nocc, naux);
       auto     &U = graph.create_zero_tensor<double, 2>("U", nocc, nocc);
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           cg::einsum("ix <- ia ; ax", &T, A, B);   // T is (occ, aux)
           cg::einsum("ij <- ix ; jx", &U, T, T);   // U is (occ, occ), through T
       }
       graph.annotate_spaces(A, {occ, virt});       // declarations may arrive after capture
       graph.annotate_spaces(B, {virt, aux});
       auto [modified, pass] = graph.apply<cg::passes::SpacePropagation>();
       // pass.num_inferred() == 2: T from A and B, then U from T, in one topological sweep.

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       cg.annotate(A, ("occ", "virt"), graph=g)
       cg.annotate(B, ("virt", "aux"), graph=g)
       prop = cg.SpacePropagation()
       pm = cg.PassManager()
       pm.add(prop)
       pm.run(g)
       assert prop.num_inferred == 2

    **Limitations**

    - Analysis-only: the pass never changes the node list, it only annotates ``TensorHandle`` objects. It always reports "not modified".
    - Annotates only graph-owned intermediates; user-owned tensors are never mutated.
    - Soundness guard: a tensor is annotated only when it has exactly one writer in this graph and is not referenced by a child sub-graph, the same rule SymmetryPropagation uses. A second writer could bind the slot to something else, and a nested loop body's writes are invisible in this graph's node list.
    - All-or-nothing per tensor: a rule that cannot resolve EVERY output slot writes nothing, since a half-filled annotation is indistinguishable from a deliberately partial one.
    - Operands that disagree about a letter are declined and counted in ``skip_reasons`` rather than reported. A cross-space conflict is a diagnosis for a validation pass to deliver, and this pass has no business failing a pipeline over it.
    - Only the four rules above fire. Every other op kind, including Gemm, BatchedGemm and the grouped ops, leaves its output unannotated.

    **Future improvements**

    - Cover the remaining structure-preserving kinds: Gemm and Gemv, the grouped and batched forms, element-wise products and quotients, and View, whose slots are its parent's.
    - Optional inheritance onto unannotated inputs, off by default, for a partially annotated program whose author accepts the weaker verdict that then follows.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_inferred
        :type: int

        Number of tensor handles this pass annotated with inferred spaces.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: SpaceRegistry

    Spaces, the relations declared between them, and the policy table keyed by their names.

    **Declared versus derived**

    A declaration is a fact the caller asserts and the registry stores. A query answers from the
    closure of those facts, and NOTHING is inferred at declaration time:

    - Scale order is stored as declared edges and queried through their transitive closure, so ``o`` < v and ``v`` < x answer ``o`` < x.
    - Containment is stored as declared edges and queried through their transitive closure, extended with the reflexive case: a space is contained in itself.
    - Disjointness is stored symmetrically as declared, and queries additionally derive it through containment on either side: if ``a`` is inside ``b`` and ``b`` is disjoint from ``c``, then ``a`` is disjoint from ``c``, and so is everything inside ``c``.

    Containment does not imply scale order; declare it if wanted.

    **Consistency**

    An inconsistent declaration throws ``std::invalid_argument``, in either order: no space may lie
    in two disjoint spaces, scale order may not cycle, and self-relations are rejected.

    **Thread safety**

    One mutex guards everything; not a hot path. References from ``space`` live as long as the registry.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

        Construct an empty registry.


    .. py:method:: register_space(space: IndexSpace) -> SpaceId

        Register a space, or recover the id of an identical one already registered.

        Never overwrites. Callers meaning different spaces need separate registries
        (``Graph::set_space_registry``).

        :param space: The space to register. Its name must not be empty.
        :returns: The id of the registered space.
        :raises Exception: if the name is empty, or if a space of that name is already registered with different content. The message names every field the two declarations disagree on and both values, through ``describe_space_conflict``.


    .. py:method:: find(name: str) -> SpaceId | None

        Look a space up by name.

        :param name: The name to look for.
        :returns: The id, or an empty optional when no space of that name is registered.


    .. py:method:: space(id: SpaceId) -> IndexSpace

        The space an id names.

        :param id: The id to resolve. Must be valid and must come from this registry.
        :returns: The registered space. The reference stays valid for the life of the registry.
        :raises Exception: if the id is invalid or out of range.


    .. py:method:: declare_less(smaller: SpaceId, larger: SpaceId) -> None

        Declare that the extent of ``smaller`` is below the extent of ``larger``.

        :param smaller: The space with the smaller scale.
        :param larger: The space with the larger scale.
        :raises Exception: if either id is invalid, if the two are the same space, or if the ordering would close a cycle with what is already declared.


    .. py:method:: declare_disjoint(first: SpaceId, second: SpaceId) -> None

        Declare that two spaces share no element.

        :param first: One space.
        :param second: The other space. The relation is symmetric.
        :raises Exception: if either id is invalid, if the two are the same space, or if some space is already known to sit inside both of them (which includes one of them sitting inside the other).


    .. py:method:: declare_contained(inner: SpaceId, outer: SpaceId) -> None

        Declare that every element of ``inner`` is an element of ``outer``.

        :param inner: The contained space.
        :param outer: The containing space.
        :raises Exception: if either id is invalid, if the two are the same space (self-containment is trivially true and asserting it signals a mistake), if the reverse containment is already declared, or if the containment would place some space inside two spaces declared disjoint.


    .. py:method:: is_less(smaller: SpaceId, larger: SpaceId) -> Tristate

        Whether ``smaller`` has a smaller scale than ``larger``.

        :param smaller: The candidate smaller space.
        :param larger: The candidate larger space.
        :returns: Yes when the closure of the declared orderings puts ``smaller`` below ``larger``, No when it puts ``larger`` below ``smaller`` or the two are the same space, Unknown when no declared ordering relates them.
        :raises Exception: if either id is invalid.


    .. py:method:: is_disjoint(first: SpaceId, second: SpaceId) -> Tristate

        Whether two spaces share no element.

        :param first: One space.
        :param second: The other space.
        :returns: Yes when disjointness was declared for the pair or follows from containment on either side, No when the two are the same space or one is known to contain the other, Unknown otherwise.
        :raises Exception: if either id is invalid.


    .. py:method:: is_contained(inner: SpaceId, outer: SpaceId) -> Tristate

        Whether every element of ``inner`` is an element of ``outer``.

        :param inner: The candidate contained space.
        :param outer: The candidate containing space.
        :returns: Yes when the two are the same space or the closure of the declared containments relates them, No when ``outer`` is instead contained in ``inner`` or the two are known disjoint, Unknown otherwise.
        :raises Exception: if either id is invalid.


    .. py:attribute:: size
        :type: int

        Number of registered spaces.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: ids
        :type: list[SpaceId]

        Every registered id, in registration order.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: SpaceTiling

    One axis of a declared TILED tensor: what it means, and how it is cut up.

    A tiled axis is described by a PARTITION, not an extent: an index space fixes the total
    (``occ`` is 4) but ``{2,2}``, ``{1,3}`` and ``{4}`` all tile it. So the two halves are
    supplied separately, and either may come from the space:

    .. code-block:: text

       {occ, virt}                        // both axes take their space's canonical tiling
       {{occ, {2, 2}}, {virt, {4, 4}}}    // this tensor tiles differently from the default
       {occ, tiles({4, 4})}               // second axis is a partition with no chemical meaning

    Converts implicitly from ``SpaceId`` so the first form needs no ceremony. There is no
    conversion from a bare tile list, so a plain ``{{2,2},{4,4}}`` keeps selecting the
    tile-sizes overload unambiguously; ``tiles`` is how a space-less axis joins a shape that
    names spaces elsewhere.

    .. seealso::

       tiles

    .. seealso::

       Graph::pin_space_tiling

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__(s: SpaceId) -> None
                   __init__(s: SpaceId, sizes: list[int]) -> None

        An axis over ``s``, tiled the way ``s`` is canonically tiled.

        :param s: The index space.


    .. py:attribute:: space
        :type: SpaceId

        The space this axis ranges over, or an invalid id when the axis names none.

    .. py:attribute:: tile_sizes
        :type: list[int]

        How the axis is cut up. Empty means "whatever ``space`` is canonically tiled as".


.. py:class:: StreamContractionFusion

    Fuse contractions that stream the same large tensor into one pass over it (the SCF "J and K from one TEI read" idiom).

    Detects groups of einsum nodes that each contract the SAME large tensor
    :math:`S` against a small operand, where every output index and every
    small-operand index is drawn from :math:`S`'s index pattern (the
    "GEMV-shaped" class: each element of :math:`S` is read exactly once and
    contributes :math:`\alpha_k\,S[\mathrm{idx}]\,W_k[\pi_k(\mathrm{idx})]` to
    :math:`C_k[\rho_k(\mathrm{idx})]`). Such contractions are memory-bandwidth
    bound - their cost is one stream of :math:`S` - so :math:`N` of them cost
    :math:`N` streams executed separately, but only ONE stream when fused: the
    replacement node walks :math:`S` once in storage order and feeds every
    member's accumulator from the same element.

    **Example (Fock build)**

    Before (two 800 MB streams of the TEI at n = 100, plus the scrambled K
    pattern running below stream speed):

    .. code-block:: text

       // J[mu,nu] += 2 * TEI[mu,nu,lam,sig] * D[lam,sig]
       // K[mu,nu] -= 1 * TEI[mu,lam,nu,sig] * D[lam,sig]

    After: one storage-order stream of TEI updating both J and K, with
    thread-private accumulators for the (small) outputs.

    Members may write different outputs (J and K) or accumulate into a shared
    one; for a shared output every member after the first must purely
    accumulate (c prefactor 1), mirroring
    ``LinearCombinationContractionFolding``'s tail rule. Conjugated members
    and repeated indices are left unfused. Complex prefactors fuse on complex
    tensors (the kernel carries element-typed alphas); on real tensors a
    prefactor with a nonzero imaginary part declines the member rather than
    silently dropping it. The streamed tensor must dominate the member
    operands and outputs in size (ratio gate below).

    **Output handling: privatization, with owner-computes chunking above the cap**

    Outputs are normally accumulated in thread-private buffers and reduced at
    the end, which requires them to stay cache-resident: with a
    ``CostModel`` carrying cache sizes the cap is derived as
    last-level-cache / threads bytes per output (so the aggregate private
    buffers fit in cache); without one a fixed fallback applies. When a
    member's output exceeds the cap, the group switches to owner-computes
    chunking instead of declining: partitioning a physical :math:`S` axis whose
    label lands in the output pins one output coordinate, so threads owning
    disjoint blocks of that axis write disjoint output slices directly - no
    private copy, no reduction, no size cap. An axis covers a member only when
    every member writing the same output puts its label in the same place
    there: members accumulating into one output through transposed patterns
    would hand each thread a row stripe and a column stripe, and the stripes of
    different threads cross. The pass records every axis that covers all
    over-cap members; the kernel partitions the highest-stride one
    (a low-stride partition turns each thread's read into a strided comb,
    measured ~5x slower than contiguous slabs). Under-cap members not covered
    by the chosen axis keep their private buffers inside the same stream. An
    over-cap member no axis can cover drops out of the group and stays an
    ordinary einsum. Groups with all outputs under the cap keep the flat
    privatized walk unconditionally - its contiguous per-thread slabs are the
    measured-fastest layout.

    **Aliasing: every operand is compared as a BUFFER**

    Fusion interleaves the members and moves every member's write to the first
    member's position, so it is only equivalent to the original order when no
    member's write is observable by another member or by any node it moves
    across. Both questions are decided on ``Graph::resolve_alias`` roots rather
    than TensorIds, because a view and its parent are different ids over one
    buffer - and in a real capture views are the common case (a DLPNO-CCSD
    iteration registers ~5800 of them against ~1200 whole tensors). A group
    declines when an output shares a buffer with the streamed tensor, with a
    weight, or with another output, and when any node between the members shares
    a buffer with one of them. The graph merges partially overlapping byte spans
    into one buffer, so two interleaved strided slices of one parent count as
    shared even though their elements are disjoint; proving such slices apart
    would need the per-view interval boxes the hazard scan builds
    (Graph::for_each_hazard_edge), which this pass does not read yet. Until it
    does, a view-heavy graph is where the pass declines most.

    **Relationship to LinearCombinationContractionFolding**

    LCCF serves the same 2J-K algebra by materializing a linear combination
    :math:`L` of the streamed tensor and contracting once - measured 2.7x over
    unfused for the Fock idiom, but it still makes ~4 passes over
    :math:`S`-sized data. This pass replaces those with a single pass. When both
    passes are registered, run this one FIRST: it consumes the pattern LCCF
    would otherwise fold.

    **Opt-in**

    Registered in ``PassManager::create_default`` (after Materialization /
    SymmetryPropagation, before GPU placement): the size and ratio gates make
    it a no-op on graphs without a qualifying stream, and it declines
    distributed operands.

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::PassManager pm; pm.add(std::make_shared<StreamContractionFusion>());
       graph.apply(pm);

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       import einsums, einsums.graph as cg
       g = cg.Graph("stream_fusion")
       with cg.capture(g):
           # Two contractions streaming the same large TEI against the small density D.
           einsums.einsum("mn <- mnls ; ls", J, TEI, D, c_pf=0.0, ab_pf=2.0)   # J += 2 * TEI * D
           einsums.einsum("mn <- mlns ; ls", K, TEI, D, c_pf=0.0, ab_pf=-1.0)  # K -= 1 * (scrambled TEI) * D
       g.apply(cg.default_pass_manager())         # StreamContractionFusion runs in the default pipeline
       # You may also add cg.StreamContractionFusion() to a manual cg.PassManager, but
       # its num_groups / num_eliminated counters are not exposed as Python properties.

    **Limitations**

    - Members must be **einsum** nodes with two inputs and one output, no repeated index in any of C/A/B (diagonal patterns decline), and non-conjugated (`conj_a`/`conj_b` decline).
    - One operand is the streamed tensor :math:`S`, the other the small :math:`W`; every C index and every W index must be drawn from :math:`S`'s index pattern (the GEMV-shaped class - each element of :math:`S` read exactly once).
    - :math:`S`, :math:`W` and C must all be **runtime** tensors (owning or view) of one dtype, each with a live ``TensorHandle::impl_fn``; a mixed-dtype member declines (the kernel casts all three to :math:`S`'s element type) and so does a tile-wise sparse operand, which has no single geometry to read.
    - A group declines when its operands share a buffer, or when a node between its members touches one (see the aliasing note above).
    - On a real dtype, `ab_prefactor` and `c_prefactor` must be real-valued; complex prefactors ride through only on complex dtypes.
    - Distributed operands decline (they belong to the InputSlicing / SUMMAExpansion communication passes).
    - Size gates: :math:`S` needs >= `kMinStreamElems` (4096) elements and must be >= `kMinSizeRatio` (8x) larger than each member's :math:`W` and output.
    - A group needs >= 2 members over the same :math:`S`, and for a shared output only the first member touching it may have `c_prefactor != 1` (contributions interleave in the one stream). An interference guard also rejects the group if any node between the first and last member writes the buffer of an operand or reads the buffer of an output.
    - Privatized members are held to `max_output_elems` (cache-derived with a cost_model, a fixed fallback without); an over-cap member is only kept if an owner-computes partition axis covers it, otherwise it drops out and stays an ordinary einsum.

    **Future improvements**

    - Admit conjugated members and repeated-index (diagonal) patterns, currently declined outright.
    - Support statically-typed `Tensor<T,Rank>` captures, not only runtime tensors.
    - Read the hazard scan's per-view interval boxes so element-disjoint slices of one parent stop counting as one buffer, which is what currently declines every candidate group in a view-heavy graph.
    - Coordinate with the communication passes so distributed streams can fuse instead of declining.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None


.. py:class:: SymmetrizedAccumulation

    Fold the CCSD permutational-symmetrization idiom :math:`r2 \mathrel{+}= s\,(t + P(t))`.

    Chemistry residuals accumulate a tensor and its index-swapped transpose into
    the same output. In the graph this appears as four nodes per site (the
    ``symacc`` helper in ``ccsd_rhf_graph_numpy_style.py``):

    .. code-block:: text

       einsum(spec, A, B)            -> tmp    // tmp = pf*(A(x)B)   (kept)
       axpby(s, tmp,  1.0, r2)                 // r2 += s*tmp
       permute("jiba <- ijab", tmp) -> tmpP    // tmpP = P(tmp)   (an involution)
       axpby(s, tmpP, 1.0, r2)                 // r2 += s*tmp^{jiba}

    The pass detects each such site (structural match + involutive permutation +
    accumulate gate + interference guard) and rewrites it (Level 1, permute
    reuse). The permute node is made to accumulate DIRECTLY into the output,
    :math:`r2 = r2 + s_2\,\alpha\,P(t)`, rebuilt as an ordinary permute node with
    beta = 1 that keeps the permute's own alpha and permutation operators, and
    the second axpby and the ``tmpP`` buffer are deleted. The first axpby
    (:math:`r2 \mathrel{+}= s_1 t`) is untouched. This drops the O(o^2 v^2) ``tmpP``
    storage and one full sweep per site with no new kernel or OpKind. See
    ``docs/symmetrized_accumulation_design.md``.

    **Example (C++)**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("symacc");
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           cg::einsum("ijab <- ia ; jb", 0.0, &tmp, 1.0, A, B);  // tmp = A (x) B
           cg::axpby(0.5, tmp, 1.0, &r2);                         // r2 += 0.5*tmp
           cg::permute("jiba <- ijab", 0.0, &tmpP, 1.0, tmp);     // tmpP = P(tmp)
           cg::axpby(0.5, tmpP, 1.0, &r2);                        // r2 += 0.5*P(tmp)
       }
       graph.apply(cg::PassManager::create_default());
       // tmpP and the second axpby are gone; the permute now does r2 += 0.5*P(tmp).

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       import einsums, einsums.graph as cg
       g = cg.Graph("symacc")
       with cg.capture(g):                                     # runtime tensors (the workload path)
           einsums.einsum("i,j,a,b <- i,a ; j,b", tmp, A, B)   # tmp = A (x) B
           einsums.linalg.axpby(0.5, tmp, 1.0, r2)             # r2 += 0.5*tmp
           einsums.permute("j,i,b,a <- i,j,a,b", tmpP, tmp)    # tmpP = P(tmp)
           einsums.linalg.axpby(0.5, tmpP, 1.0, r2)            # r2 += 0.5*P(tmp)
       sa = cg.SymmetrizedAccumulation()
       pm = cg.PassManager(); pm.add(sa)
       g.apply(pm)                                             # or cg.default_pass_manager()
       # sa.num_rewritten  -> 1   (getters are properties, not methods)

    In the default pipeline (after DeadNodeElimination, before ElementWiseFusion —
    which would otherwise compose the two axpby and hide the pattern). Recurses
    into loop bodies: the CCSD residual is captured as a loop body.

    **Limitations**

    - The source and the output must hold one element type, and a real output is never folded under a complex factor.
    - The source may be read through a view, but the output and ``tmpP`` may not be views: the folded node would write the output's parent storage, and ``tmpP`` must be storage no other id names. The source may not share the output's storage either, since the folded node reads one while writing the other.
    - Level 1 keeps the transpose (the accumulating permute), so REPLAY TIME is ~compute-neutral (a few %, transpose-bound). The win is eliminating the O(o^2 v^2) ``tmpP`` buffer (peak memory), not compute.
    - Detection requires the exact structure: an involutive overwrite permute whose output is consumed, within the generation this permute starts (up to the buffer's next overwrite), by exactly one accumulating axpby, plus a sibling accumulating axpby reading the un-permuted source into the same output from the source's own generation. Scratch REUSED across sites (the CCSD body recycles one tmp/tmpP for every term) matches per generation; an interference guard rejects sites where another node observes the half-symmetrized output.

    **Future improvements**

    - Level 2: a fused single-sweep ``SymmetrizedAxpby`` kernel (:math:`r2 \mathrel{+}= s_1 t + s_2 P(t)` in one pass), profiling-gated — drops the second sweep as well, not just the buffer.
    - Generalize beyond involutive permutations and allow :math:`s_1 \neq s_2`.
    - Accept a view as the output: the folded node reaches it through its impl, so what is left is proving the interference guard sees every other view of it.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_candidates
        :type: int

        Structural matches: permute (involutive, overwrite) whose output feeds,
        within the generation this permute starts, exactly one accumulating
        axpby into an output that a sibling accumulating axpby also feeds from
        the un-permuted source's generation.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_matched
        :type: int

        Candidates that also pass the interference guard (no other node observes
        the half-symmetrized output or rewrites the source mid-fold) - the sites
        that are safe to fold.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: num_rewritten
        :type: int

        Sites actually rewritten (num_matched that also passed the dtype and
        operand gates). Each eliminates one axpby and one tmpP buffer.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: SymmetryPropagation

    Phase 3 of the symmetry plan: infer symmetry on graph intermediates.

    Walks the graph in topological order. For each node that produces a
    tensor, checks whether the output can be proven symmetric / antisymmetric
    / Hermitian from the inputs' declared descriptors, and if so tags the
    output ``TensorHandle`` and pushes the descriptor back to the backing
    tensor (so subsequent ``graph.execute()`` benefits from the rank-2 BLAS
    dispatch in LinearAlgebra/SymmetryDispatch.hpp).

    **Current rules**

    - **Scale**: ``C = α·A`` inherits A's descriptor (scaling preserves symmetry).
    - **Axpy / Axpby / sum of same-descriptor inputs**: ``C = α·A + β·B`` where A and B share a descriptor inherits that descriptor.
    - **Permute / Transpose** of a rank-2 symmetric tensor with the natural swap-permutation remains symmetric.
    - **Einsum ``ki,kj->ij`` (pattern ``AᵀA``)**: the product of A with its own transpose is always symmetric (for real) / Hermitian (for complex) regardless of A.

    Rules are deliberately conservative, only cases that hold unconditionally
    get tagged. Everything else is left untagged, which is the safe default.

    **Scope**

    Only tags graph-owned intermediates (``is_intermediate == true``).
    User-owned tensors are never mutated by this pass.

    In the default pipeline (after Materialization, so the backing tensors exist,
    and before GPU placement, so downstream passes and executions see the inferred
    symmetry).

    **Example (C++)**

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::Graph graph("symmetry_propagation");
       {
           cg::CaptureGuard const capture(graph);
           // T = AᵀA is provably symmetric; when T is a graph-owned intermediate
           // (e.g. produced by ContractionPlanning or declare_tensor) the pass tags it.
           cg::einsum("ij <- ki ; kj", 0.0, &T, 1.0, A, A);   // T symmetric
           cg::einsum("il <- ij ; jl", 0.0, &C, 1.0, T, B);   // consumer sees the tag
       }
       graph.apply(cg::PassManager::create_default());        // SymmetryPropagation runs here
       // T's handle is tagged symmetric, enabling the rank-2 BLAS dispatch at execute().

    **Example (Python)**

    .. code-block:: py

       import einsums, einsums.graph as cg
       g = cg.Graph("symmetry_propagation")
       with cg.capture(g):
           einsums.einsum("ij <- ki ; kj", T, A, A)   # T = AᵀA (symmetric intermediate)
           einsums.einsum("il <- ij ; jl", C, T, B)
       sp = cg.SymmetryPropagation()
       pm = cg.PassManager(); pm.add(sp)
       g.apply(pm)                                    # or cg.default_pass_manager()
       # sp.num_inferred -> number of tensors tagged (getter is a property, not a method)

    **Limitations**

    - Analysis-only: the pass never changes the node list. It only tags the ``TensorHandle`` objects and pushes descriptors to the backing tensors.
    - Tags only graph-owned intermediates (``is_intermediate``); user-owned tensors are never mutated.
    - Soundness guard: a tensor is tagged only when it has exactly one writer in this graph and is not referenced by a child sub-graph (a nested loop/conditional body could rewrite it invisibly), so nothing can invalidate the tag later.
    - Only four rules fire, all unconditional: Scale (inherit), Axpy/Axpby whose two inputs carry **identical** descriptors, rank-2 Permute/Transpose with the swap permutation and a zero destination prefactor on a symmetric/Hermitian input, and the self-contraction einsum (same tensor in both operand slots, rank-2 x rank-2 -> rank-2 with exactly one link index).
    - The permute rule is rank-2 only and propagates only symmetric/Hermitian pair descriptors (higher-rank descriptors can encode cross-axis invariants that do not carry through every permute).
    - Everything not provably structured is left untagged (the safe default).

    **Future improvements**

    - Add rules for more op kinds: general einsum symmetry inference, antisymmetric outputs, propagation through gemm / BatchedGemm nodes.
    - Relax the rank-2-only permute rule to higher-rank symmetry descriptors once the cross-axis invariants are tracked.


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

    .. py:attribute:: num_inferred
        :type: int

        Number of tensor handles this pass tagged with an inferred descriptor.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: TensorBacking

    The storage behind a tensor the graph reads, or writes, but never holds whole.

    ``kind`` and ``location`` are what a saved graph records, with ``write``. ``dims`` and
    ``dtype`` describe what the storage holds and are checked against the tensor when the backing
    is annotated, so a mismatch is reported where it is made rather than at the first replay.
    ``read_slab`` and ``write_slab`` are rebuilt at load by the registered ``BackingKind`` and
    never written to a file.

    A READ backing names storage that already holds the tensor: an input the graph only reads. A
    WRITE backing names storage the graph fills: the tensor is an output the graph produces, and
    may read back after producing it. A write backing is completed by the tensor it is annotated
    on, through ``prepare``, since the storage may not exist yet to describe it. An UPDATE backing
    is a write backing whose storage also holds the value each replay starts from: the graph reads
    the tensor, writes its next value, and the storage is switched to that value at the end of the
    replay (``update``, ``publish``), together with every other update backing of the graph that
    shares its ``publish_scope``.

    Built by the module that knows the storage, as ``tensor_io::etn_backing`` builds one for a
    ``.etn`` entry, and handed to ``Graph::annotate_backing``.

    .. seealso::

       Graph::annotate_backing

    .. seealso::

       register_backing_kind


    .. py:method:: describe() -> str

        The kind and location in one phrase, for diagnostics.

        :returns: Text such as ``tensor_io.etn (entry=B_vv, path=ints.etn)``.


    .. py:attribute:: dtype
        :type: str | None

        The stored tensor's element type by name, as a ``dtype=`` argument spells it.

        .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: kind
        :type: str

        The registered kind's name, qualified as in ``"tensor_io.etn"``.

    .. py:attribute:: location
        :type: list[tuple[str, str]]

        Key/value pairs that name the storage within its kind, sorted by key, as in
        ``{{"entry", "B_vv"}, {"path", "ints.etn"}}``.

    .. py:attribute:: dims
        :type: list[int]

        The stored tensor's extents.

    .. py:attribute:: write
        :type: bool

        Whether the graph writes the storage (an output it produces) rather than only reading it.

    .. py:attribute:: update
        :type: bool

        Whether the graph also reads the value the storage holds when a replay starts: an UPDATE
        backing, set with ``write``. ``read_slab`` reads that value, ``write_slab`` writes the
        next one beside it, and ``publish`` makes the next one current once the replay has made
        every access. A crash before the publish leaves the storage holding the previous value.

    .. py:attribute:: publish_scope
        :type: str

        Which update backings publish together: those of one graph with the same non-empty scope
        are published by one call, so a crash leaves all of them at the values the replay started
        from or all at the values it wrote. A kind sets it to what one atomic step can cover, as
        ``tensor_io.etn`` sets the file's path. Empty: published alone.


.. py:class:: ThcFactorization

    Offer a four-index tensor as a fitted chain over two grid indices.

    .. seealso::

       ThcFactorization.hpp for the mathematics and for what chemistry calls it

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__(tag: str, three_index: Any, collocation: Any, bound: float = 0., drop_threshold: float = einsums::compute_graph::ThcFactorization::default_drop_threshold, name: str = "Thc") -> None
                   __init__(tag: str, three_index: Any, collocations: list[Any], bound: float = 0., drop_threshold: float = einsums::compute_graph::ThcFactorization::default_drop_threshold, name: str = "Thc") -> None

        The same, taking runtime-rank tensors. This is the overload Python gets.

        .. note::

           The drop-threshold default is spelled with its class qualifier because the binding
           generator copies a default argument through verbatim, and an unqualified
           class-scope constant does not resolve where the generated code spells it.


    .. py:staticmethod:: for_amplitude(tag: str, amplitude: Any, collocations: list[Any], bound: float = 0., drop_threshold: float = einsums::compute_graph::ThcFactorization::default_drop_threshold, name: str = "ThcAmplitude") -> ThcFactorization

        The same, taking runtime-rank tensors. This is the overload Python gets.

        :param tag: The provenance tag this claims.
        :param amplitude: The very tensor the caller tags.
        :param collocations: One matrix per axis, or one for every axis.
        :param bound: The relative error asserted, or zero to take the option.
        :param drop_threshold: See ``default_drop_threshold``.
        :param name: The provider's name.
        :returns: The provider.


    .. py:staticmethod:: for_three_index(tag: str, three_index: Any, collocations: list[Any], bound: float = 0., drop_threshold: float = einsums::compute_graph::ThcFactorization::default_drop_threshold, name: str = "ThcThreeIndex") -> ThcFactorization

        The same, taking runtime-rank tensors. This is the overload Python gets.

        :param tag: The provenance tag this claims.
        :param three_index: The very tensor the caller tags.
        :param collocations: One matrix per basis axis, or one for both.
        :param bound: The relative error asserted, or zero to take the option.
        :param drop_threshold: See ``default_drop_threshold``.
        :param name: The provider's name.
        :returns: The provider.


    .. py:method:: report_residual_into(residual: Any, reference: Any) -> None

        The same, taking runtime-rank tensors. This is the overload Python gets.

        :param residual: A rank-1 tensor for the squared residual.
        :param reference: A rank-1 tensor for the squared norm of what was fitted.


    .. py:staticmethod:: grid_space_name() -> str

        The index space the fitted factors' grid axes carry.

        :returns: ``"grid"``.


    .. py:staticmethod:: grid_dim_symbol() -> str

        The symbolic extent a grid axis goes by.

        :returns: ``"ngrid"``.


    .. py:staticmethod:: register_grid_space(graph: Graph) -> SpaceId

        Register the grid space in ``graph's`` own registry, if it is not there already.

        A space is a statement about a graph rather than about a provider, and ``propose`` is
        handed a const graph precisely so a provider cannot make one. So a caller registers it,
        and this is the one call that does: idempotent for a repeated identical declaration, as
        ``SpaceRegistry::register_space`` is.

        :param graph: The graph whose registry gains the space.
        :returns: The space's id in that registry.


    .. py:staticmethod:: residual_param_name(provider: str, tensor: str) -> str

        The parameter the squared residual of the fit is reported under.

        Divided by ``reference_param_name`` and square-rooted, this is the RELATIVE least-squares
        residual of the grid fit against the three-index tensor it was fitted from. Per bind and
        not saved, because it is a property of the grid and the integrals currently bound rather
        than of the structure.

        :param provider: The provider's ``name``.
        :param tensor: The tagged tensor's name.
        :returns: The key to read out of the graph's parameter table after an execute.


    .. py:staticmethod:: reference_param_name(provider: str, tensor: str) -> str

        The parameter the squared norm of the three-index tensor is reported under.

        :param provider: The provider's ``name``.
        :param tensor: The tagged tensor's name.
        :returns: The key. See ``residual_param_name`` for what the two are for.


    .. py:attribute:: epsilon
        :type: float

        The tolerance this provider asserts, taking the constructor over the option.


        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: drop_threshold
        :type: float

        The threshold below which this provider drops a grid direction.


        (read-only)


.. py:class:: WaggleBridge

    An observer that labels the profiler's zones with what the graph was doing in them.

    Every executor opens a zone per node while the profiler records. This adds to it the graph and
    the node it belongs to, and each read, write and publish of a backing the node made, with its
    bytes (``read t2``, ``write t2_next``), so the live viewer and the saved profile name the graph
    node behind a zone and the storage traffic inside it. Without the profiler recording, it does
    nothing.

    .. code-block:: text

       g.set_observer(cg.WaggleBridge())

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0


    .. py:method:: __init__() -> None

        A bridge to the profiler this process records with.



.. py:class:: Workspace

    Owns tensors that persist across multiple Graphs and Pipelines.

    Workspace is the outermost scoping layer for tensor lifetime:

    - Workspace: cross-computation (ERI, MO coefficients, basis data)
    - Pipeline: cross-stage (Fock matrix, density, amplitudes)
    - Graph: single-computation intermediates (temporaries)

    Tensors declared via Workspace use deferred allocation: they have
    valid metadata (name, dims, dtype) but no backing data until the
    MaterializationPass runs. This allows the DistributionPlanningPass
    to decide how to partition large tensors across MPI ranks before
    any memory is allocated.

    **Example**

    .. code-block:: text

       namespace cg = einsums::compute_graph;

       cg::Workspace ws("h2o");

       // Declare tensors, no data allocated yet
       auto &eri = ws.declare_tensor<double, 4>("ERI", nao, nao, nao, nao);
       auto &C   = ws.declare_zero_tensor<double, 2>("C", nao, nmo);

       // Use in a pipeline
       cg::Pipeline scf("scf");
       scf.set_workspace(ws);
       {
           auto &stage = scf.add_stage("compute");
           cg::CaptureGuard guard(stage);
           cg::einsum("ijkl;kl->ij", 0.0, &F, 1.0, eri, D);
       }

       scf.apply(pm);   // Passes decide distribution, then materialize
       scf.execute();    // Computation runs on materialized tensors

       // eri and C survive, reuse in MP2, CCSD, etc.


    .. py:method:: __init__(name: str) -> None

    .. py:method:: declare_runtime_tensor(tensor_name: str, dims: list[int]) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Runtime-rank analog of declare_tensor().

        Same deferred-allocation lifecycle as the typed version (no data until
        materialize_fn fires) but the rank is carried at runtime. Suitable for
        Python-bound workflows or any workflow that wants a single dispatch
        surface across ranks.


    .. py:method:: declare_zero_runtime_tensor(tensor_name: str, dims: list[int]) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Runtime-rank analog of declare_zero_tensor().


    .. py:method:: declare_random_runtime_tensor(tensor_name: str, dims: list[int]) -> GeneralRuntimeTensor

        Runtime-rank analog of declare_random_tensor().


    .. py:method:: materialize_all() -> None

        Materialize and initialize all deferred tensors.


    .. py:attribute:: name
        :type: str

        (read-only)

    .. py:attribute:: size
        :type: int

        Number of declared tensors.


        (read-only)


Functions
---------

.. py:function:: batched_gemm(alpha: float, a_list: list[Any], b_list: list[Any], beta: float, c_list: list[Any], trans_a: bool = False, trans_b: bool = False) -> None

    Emit one `blas::gemm_batch` over a batch of independent GEMMs: ``C_i = alpha * op(A_i) op(B_i) + beta * C_i`` for every i.

    The GEMMBatching pass already fuses independent 2D einsums into a single
    BatchedGemm node, so the fused form was reachable without this. What it was
    not reachable *cheaply*: the caller had to emit one node per GEMM and let
    the pass collapse them, and capture costs on the order of tens of
    microseconds a node. A DLPNO-MP2 residual emitted 8112 nodes for the passes
    to fuse into 33, spending more time building the graph than replaying it.
    This lets a caller who already knows the batch say so directly.

    Every member must share m, n, k, the transpose flags, the element type and
    the leading dimensions, which is what `gemm_batch` takes as scalars. The
    prefactors are shared too, so a per-member prefactor has to be folded into
    the operands beforehand. Mismatches throw here rather than at execute time.

    Outside capture this executes immediately, so the same call works eagerly.

    :param alpha: Prefactor on op(A_i) op(B_i), shared by the batch.
    :param a_list: Left operands. Must all be rank 2 with identical dims.
    :param b_list: Right operands, same length as ``a_list``.
    :param beta: Prefactor on C_i. Non-zero means every C_i is read as well as written, which the node records as a dependency.
    :param c_list: Destinations, same length as ``a_list``. Must be distinct tensors: `gemm_batch` gives no ordering between members, so two members sharing a destination race.
    :param trans_a: Transpose each A_i.
    :param trans_b: Transpose each B_i.


.. py:function:: batched_gemm_blocked(alpha: float, a_list: list[Any], b_list: list[Any], beta: float, c_base: Any, c_offsets: list[int], c_rows: int, c_cols: int, trans_a: bool = False, trans_b: bool = False) -> None

    ``batched_gemm`` where the destinations are uniform-shaped blocks of a
    single tensor, described by their offsets rather than enumerated as views.

    The list form needs one tensor object per member, and building those is not
    free: a caller whose destinations are column ranges of one store has to
    materialize a view apiece, which in a local-correlation method is tens of
    thousands of them. On the DLPNO port's overlap build that is 32948 view
    constructions on the Python side and 32948 more slot registrations here,
    together about half the phase, and none of it is arithmetic.

    ``c_offsets`` gives each block's first element as an offset into ``c_base``,
    in elements. Every block is ``c_rows`` by ``c_cols`` and inherits the base's
    leading dimension, which is what makes them expressible this way: a column
    range of a column-major tensor has the parent's ``lda``.

    A and B stay lists, because the callers that want this have destinations
    that repeat one tensor and sources that do not. Passing a list of existing
    tensors is cheap; it is *constructing* the views that is not.

    One further difference from the list form, and it is a correctness one: the
    node declares a single output, ``c_base`` itself, so a later read of the whole
    base is ordered against this write. The list form declares the views, and
    the graph does not know a view write touches its parent, so that ordering
    has to be forced with a graph boundary.


.. py:function:: custom(label: str, executor: Callable[[Any], None], pure: bool = False) -> None
                 custom(label: str, executor: Callable[[Any], None], target: Any, pure: bool = False) -> None

    No-dependency custom node, Python-friendly overload.

    Records a graph node that runs ``executor`` with no declared
    input/output tensor dependencies. Outside a capture context the
    executor runs immediately. Use the tensor-tagged overload below for
    read-modify-write patterns where the optimizer should see the
    dependency.

    A node with no tensors acts only through its effects, so it is recorded with
    ``opaque_effects`` unless ``pure`` is true (see ``PureTag``).

    .. code-block:: text

       cg::custom("debug_print", []() { fmt::print("hello\n"); });

    :param label: Human-readable name for profiling and debugging.
    :param executor: Callable that performs the operation.
    :param pure: True to declare the operation pure.


.. py:function:: diis_add_pair(accelerator: DiisAccelerator, amplitude: Any, step: Any) -> None

    Register one ``(amplitude, step)`` pair with a DIIS accelerator.

    ``amplitude`` is the tensor the iteration updates and ``step`` is the update
    the body computed, which DIIS also uses as the error vector. Both are bound
    by storage, so they must outlive the accelerator, and every pair has to be
    added before the first step - the snapshots are shaped for the pair list.

    This is bookkeeping, not an operation: it records nothing into an active
    capture and works the same inside and outside one.


.. py:function:: diis_step(accelerator: DiisAccelerator) -> None

    Take one DIIS step over an accelerator's registered pairs.

    Outside capture this extrapolates immediately, which is how a host-side
    convergence loop uses it. Inside capture it records one node whose executor
    holds the accelerator, so a replay of the graph steps the same history; the
    node reads every amplitude and step tensor and writes every amplitude, which
    is what orders it against the body around it.


.. py:function:: duplicate_materializations(graph: Graph) -> list[str]

    Tensors with more than one Materialize node, anywhere in ``graph's`` tree.

    A tensor's storage is allocated once. Two lifecycles for one buffer survive only because
    ``materialize_fn`` happens to be idempotent, which is a property of today's hook rather than a
    contract, and the extra node carries a dependency edge that serializes work against itself for
    nothing. Two passes each emitting one is how it happens, and no numeric oracle can see it: both
    allocations produce the same buffer and the answer does not move.

    Keyed by NAME rather than by id, because a sub-graph carries its own id for the parent's buffer
    and a name is the one handle both spellings share, which is what ``already_materialized_in``
    inside the pass already relies on.

    :param graph: The graph to audit; loop bodies, conditional branches and setup bodies are included.
    :returns: The names, sorted, of every tensor named by more than one Materialize node.


.. py:function:: eager_einsum_intermediate_limit(element_size: int) -> int

    The most elements an eager einsum over several operands lets a partial product hold.

    A captured einsum is not limited: its partial products are graph intermediates, and the graph's
    ``AxisTiling`` pass builds one a block at a time when it is too large to hold. An eager einsum
    has no such pass, so its partial products are held to what ``--einsums:max-memory`` allows.

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

    :param element_size: The bytes of one element.
    :returns: The limit, or zero for none.


.. py:function:: fixed(extent: int) -> SpaceDim

    An axis whose size is a number and whose meaning is nothing.

    A DIIS history depth or a fixed block count is a real thing to want in a shape that
    otherwise names spaces, and it must NOT pick up a dim symbol: a literal axis is one a
    bind may not move, which is exactly what "fixed" says.

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

    :param extent: The size.
    :returns: The dimension.


.. py:function:: global_factorization_registry() -> FactorizationRegistry

    The process-wide provider registry.

    .. versionadded:: 2.0.0

    :returns: The registry. Never null.


.. py:function:: global_space_registry() -> SpaceRegistry

    The process-wide space registry.

    Register spaces and declare their relations during startup or setup; queries then run from
    anywhere. The registry's own mutex makes the concurrent case safe rather than fast.

    :returns: The single registry shared by every graph in this process.


.. py:function:: grouped_batched_gemm(alpha: float, a_list: list[Any], b_list: list[Any], beta: float, c_list: list[Any], trans_a: bool = False, trans_b: bool = False) -> None

    Emit one `blas::gemm_batch_grouped` over independent GEMMs whose shapes need NOT agree: ``C_i = alpha * op(A_i) op(B_i) + beta * C_i``.

    The same signature as ``batched_gemm``, minus its central restriction.
    Members are sorted into shape groups here, at capture, and the whole set
    then runs under ONE OpenMP region instead of one per shape.

    That is the entire point, and it is not a small one. Entering a region costs
    tens of microseconds on a wide team, which is comparable to a GEMM of the
    size local-correlation methods produce. Measured on a DLPNO-MP2 iteration
    whose batched GEMMs want 16 ms of arithmetic, issuing them as 754 uniform
    calls took 45 ms and issuing them as one grouped call took 16.7. The same
    batch also stopped caring how finely it was split: the effective rate held
    flat at ~230 GFLOPS across 120, 448 and 754 shape classes, where the looped
    form fell 146 to 80.

    Grouping is by (m, n, k, lda, ldb, ldc); the prefactors and transpose flags
    are shared by the whole call, as they are for ``batched_gemm``. Groups are
    numbered in order of first appearance and members keep their relative order
    inside a group, but members of DIFFERENT shapes are reordered against each
    other. That is within contract for the same reason the uniform form gives no
    ordering between members: they must be independent.
